\displaystyle=\frac{f_{a_{j}}(X_{i})/f_{0}(X_{i})}{\sum_{k\in Q_{j}}f_{a_{j}}(X_{k})/f_{0}(X_{k})}=\frac{h(X_{i},a_{j})}{\sum_{k\in Q_{j}}h(X_{k},a_{j})},

\displaystyle h(x,a)

\displaystyle=f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x).

In addition,

\displaystyle M_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0\,,\,\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)]=\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}P_{\pi}[i=I_{j}\,,\,\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}P_{\pi}\Big[i=I_{j}\,,\,(\rho+1)h(X_{i},a_{j})<\sum_{k\in Q_{j}}h(X_{k},a_{j})\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}P_{\pi}\big[i=I_{j}\,,\,\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(X_{k},a_{j})>h(X_{i},a_{j})\}\geq\rho\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(\varepsilon_{k},a_{j})>h(X_{i},a_{j})\}\geq\rho\right],

(35)

where the
\varepsilon_{k}
’s are i.i.d.
\sim f_{0}
, and in the last line
i=i_{j}\in Q_{j}
is chosen arbitrarily by symmetry.

Consider the first case, when Assumption
1
1
holds. We see that
\varepsilon_{k}>X_{i}
implies
h(\varepsilon_{k},a_{j})>h(X_{i},a_{j})
, so that the last display can be further lower bounded by

\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>\varepsilon_{k}>X_{i}\}\geq\rho\right].

By Lemma
S-7
, provided
\rho
satisfies the condition of the lemma, we have

P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>\varepsilon_{k}>X_{i}\}\geq\rho\right]=F_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1),

so that continuing the inequalities,

\displaystyle M_{\rho}/s_{n}

\displaystyle\geq s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}F_{a_{j}}\left(a_{n}^{*}\right)+o(1)=\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1),

(36)

where
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
is defined by (
22
).
This gives the lower bound

\displaystyle\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq

\displaystyle\left([\Lambda_{n}({\bf a})+o(1)](1-\eta)\wedge\frac{\rho[\Lambda_{n}({\bf a})+o(1)](1-\eta)}{1+\rho[\Lambda_{n}({\bf a})+o(1)](1-\eta)}\right)

\displaystyle\times(1-e^{-c\eta^{2}[\Lambda_{n}({\bf a})+o(1)](1-\eta)s_{n}}).

Now using that for all
x\in[0,1]
,
A,y>0
, we have

[x\wedge(yx/(1+yx))](1-e^{-Ax})\geq x+0\wedge(1-1/(1+yx)-x)-xe^{-Ax}\geq x-1/y-1/(Ae),

we deduce

\displaystyle\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq

\displaystyle[\Lambda_{n}({\bf a})+o(1)](1-\eta)-\rho^{-1}-1/(c\eta^{2}s_{n}e).

Let us set
\rho=\lfloor(n/(3s_{n}))\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\rfloor\to\infty
, an admissible choice for applying Lemma
S-7
. Further setting
\eta=s_{n}^{-1/4}
, one gets

\varliminf_{n}\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\Lambda_{n}({\bf a})+o(1).

This proves the lower bound.

When instead Assumption
1
2
holds, we note that
h(x,a)=h(-x,a)
is increasing in
\lvert x\rvert
for
a\neq 0
, hence returning to (
35
) we see

\displaystyle M_{\rho}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\big[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>\lvert\varepsilon_{k}\rvert>\lvert X_{i}\rvert\}\geq\rho\big].

Lemma
S-7
tells us that in this case we have

P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>\lvert\varepsilon_{k}\rvert>\lvert X_{i}\rvert\}\geq\rho\right]=1-2\overline{F}_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1)=2F_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})-1+o(1),

yielding

M_{\rho}/s_{n}\geq 2\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-1+o(1),

(37)

and we finish the proof as under the other assumption.

8.2
Upper bound

If a subsequence
n_{j}
is such that
\Lambda_{n_{j}}\to 1
, then the trivial test
\varphi=0
has
\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)=1=\Lambda_{n_{j}}+o(1)=2\Lambda_{n_{j}}-1+o(1)
. We may therefore limit our attention to a subsequence on which
\Lambda_{n_{j}}
is bounded away from 1. Relabel
n_{j}
as
n
.

Define

\varphi_{i}(X)=\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*}\}.

Write
V
and
S
for the number of false discoveries and true discoveries, respectively, made by
\varphi
.

By Markov’s inequality, for any
c_{n}>0
the number of false discoveries satisfies

P_{\theta}(V>c_{n}s_{n})\leq c_{n}^{-1}s_{n}^{-1}E[V]=c_{n}^{-1}(2(n-s_{n})/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}).

This latter expression tends to zero by Assumption
1
if
c_{n}
tends to zero slowly enough, yielding that
V=o_{P}(s_{n})
.
Similarly, using that
\Var(S)\leq E[S]\leq s_{n}
and applying Chebyshev’s inequality, the number of true discoveries satisfies

P_{\theta}[\lvert S-E_{\theta}S\rvert\geq s_{n}^{3/4}]\leq s_{n}^{-1/2}.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an event of probability tending to one on which
V
and
S
are suitably bounded.

Under Assumption
1
1
, for
a\neq 0
we have by symmetry

P_{\theta_{i}=a}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*})=\overline{F}_{a}(a_{n}^{*})+F_{a}(-a_{n}^{*})=\overline{F}_{a}(a_{n}^{*})+\overline{F}_{-a}(a_{n}^{*}).

Noting that by the assumed monotonicity
\overline{F}_{-\lvert a\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})\leq\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*})\to 0
because
a_{n}^{*}\to\infty
, we deduce that

P_{\theta_{i}=a}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*})=\overline{F}_{\lvert a\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1)

where the errors
o(1)
tend to zero uniformly in
a
. We thus calculate

\displaystyle E_{\theta}[S]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}P_{\theta_{i}}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*})=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\left(\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j\leq s_{n}}\left(\overline{F}_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1)\right)=s_{n}[1-\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1)],\

where we have used that
S_{\theta}
can be enumerated as
i_{1},\dots,i_{s_{n}}
with
\lvert\theta_{i_{j}}\rvert\geq a_{j}>0
.

The combined risk of
\varphi
is then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}

\displaystyle(\theta,\varphi)=E_{\theta}\frac{V}{V+S}+E_{\theta}\frac{s_{n}-S}{s_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq P(\mathcal{A}^{c})+\frac{o(s_{n})}{o(s_{n})+s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1))-s_{n}^{3/4}}+\frac{s_{n}(\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1))}{s_{n}}=\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1),

yielding the desired upper bound.

Under Assumption
1
2
, the same calculations hold, except that now
P_{\theta_{i}}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*})=2\overline{F}_{\theta_{i}}(a_{n}^{*})
, hence (again using the assumed monotonicity)
s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}P_{\theta_{i}}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*})\geq s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{j\leq s_{n}}2\overline{F}_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})=1-(2\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-1+o(1))
, which gives the desired upper bound in this case as well.

S-1
Interpretation and verification of Assumption
1

S-1.1
Verification of Assumption
1
in Subbotin case

Assumption
1
relies on a choice of a pair
(a_{n}^{*},\delta_{n})
. The next remark provides an example of such a choice, which also allows us to approximate
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
.

Remark S-6
.

If (
16
) and (
17
) hold for some
a_{n}^{*}\to\infty
and
\delta_{n}\to 0
, then for
b_{n}^{*}=\overline{F}_{0}^{-1}(s_{n}/n)
we necessarily have

\displaystyle(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(b_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})

\displaystyle\to\infty,

(S-1)

\displaystyle(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(b_{n}^{*}+\delta_{n})

\displaystyle\to 0,

(S-2)

since
\overline{F}_{0}
is continuous and monotone, and since by the intermediate value theorem we must have
a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}<b_{n}^{*}<a_{n}^{*}
for
n
large enough.
Moreover, since the
F_{a}
are uniformly Lipschitz,

\frac{1}{s_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}F_{a_{j}}(b_{n}^{*})=\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1).

(S-3)

We may therefore always choose
a_{n}^{*}=b_{n}^{*}+o(1)
, and use (
S-3
) to approximate
\Lambda_{n}
.

Similar reasoning indicates that we may replace any valid
a_{n}^{*}
by
a_{n}^{*}+\kappa_{n}
if
\kappa_{n}=o(1)
, lending some flexibility which aids in condition (
24
).
In the following result we locally introduce the notation
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}=(\zeta\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/\zeta}
since choosing
a_{n}^{*}
slightly larger than
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}
is helpful for example in Theorem
5
.

Lemma S-1
.

Assumption
1
1
holds in the Subbotin location model
X_{i}=a+\varepsilon_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and Assumption
1
2
holds in the Subbotin scale model
X_{i}=(1+|a|)^{1/2}\varepsilon_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, for a noise
\varepsilon_{i}
i.i.d. distributed as the Subbotin density
\phi_{\zeta}
defined by (
20
), with
\zeta>1
.
In both of these cases, Assumption
1
holds for the pair
(a_{n}^{*},\delta_{n})
for
a_{n}^{*}=\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}:=(\zeta\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/\zeta}
and
\delta_{n}=(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-\upsilon}
for any
\upsilon\in(0,1-1/\zeta)
but also for all the pairs
(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n},\delta_{n}+\kappa_{n})
with any positive sequence
\kappa_{n}\to 0
. In addition, we have

\displaystyle\frac{n}{s_{n}}\overline{F}_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta_{n})

\displaystyle\gtrsim(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp\big(c(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1-1/\zeta-\upsilon}\big)\to\infty

(S-4)

\displaystyle\frac{n}{s_{n}}\overline{F}_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n})

\displaystyle\lesssim(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp(-\kappa_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{\zeta-1})\to 0.

(S-5)

Remark S-7
.

In the Subbotin models, some of our conditions can be made more explicit using Lemma
S-1
(recall that
\zeta=2
is the Gaussian case), defining
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}
as in the lemma:

•

Theorem
6
, condition on
B=(B_{n})_{n}
: the inequality
B_{n}^{2}\leq(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})/3
holds if
B_{n}^{2}\leq\exp((\log(n/s_{n}))^{\upsilon})
for some
\upsilon\in(0,1-1/\zeta)
, by applying (
S-4
) and choosing the pair
(a_{n}^{*},\delta_{n})=(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta},(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-\upsilon^{\prime}})
for
\upsilon^{\prime}<1-1/\zeta-\upsilon
. Under polynomial sparsity (
12
), the condition
B_{n}^{2}\leq\exp((\log n)^{\upsilon})
suffices.

•

Theorem
5
, condition (
24
) for a suitable choice of
a_{n}^{*},\delta_{n}
:
Recall that condition (
24
) is satisfied if for
n
large
\Lambda_{n}
is bounded away from 1 and
(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\geq 1
, and if both
\alpha_{n}
and
(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*})/\alpha_{n}
tend to zero. The latter can be achieved with any
\alpha_{n}\to 0
satisfying
\log(1/\alpha_{n})=o((\log(n/s_{n}))^{1-1/\zeta})
: to see this, apply (
S-5
) for the pair
(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n},\delta_{n}+\kappa_{n})=((\zeta\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/\zeta}+\kappa_{n},\delta_{n}+\kappa_{n})
with
\kappa_{n}\asymp\log(1/\alpha_{n})/\log(n/s_{n})^{1-1/\zeta}
. Under polynomial sparsity (
12
), the condition reduces to
\log(1/\alpha_{n})=o((\log n)^{1-1/\zeta})
, so that no further knowledge of
s_{n}
is required to define the BH procedure. Other regimes, such as
s_{n}\asymp n/(\log n)^{d}
, may also be permitted for suitably chosen
\alpha_{n}
, slightly relaxing the polynomial sparsity assumption.

•

Theorem
5
, conclusion:
Combining the previous point with the conclusion (
25
) of Theorem
5
(and recalling that the
\overline{F}_{a}
are uniformly Lipschitz) leads to the bound

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)-s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n})-s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})+\alpha_{n}+\exp\Big(-\frac{(1-\Lambda_{n}(\theta))^{2}}{32}s_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\log(1/\alpha_{n})}{\log(n/s_{n})^{1-1/\zeta}}+\alpha_{n}+\exp\Big(-\frac{(1-\Lambda_{n}(\theta))^{2}}{32}s_{n}\Big),

with a (close to) optimal choice of
\alpha_{n}=(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-(1-1/\zeta)}
, giving a convergence rate to
s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})
of order at most
\frac{\log\log(n/s_{n})}{\log(n/s_{n})^{1-1/\zeta}}+e^{-cs_{n}}
for some constant
c>0
.

Proof.

In view of Remark
3
, it suffices to verify (
16
), (
17
) and either (
18
) or (
19
).
Let us begin by verifying the conditions on the null model, which for both the location and scale Subbotin models has
f_{0},F_{0}
and
\overline{F}_{0}
equal to
\phi_{\zeta},\Phi_{\zeta}
and
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}
respectively. By Lemma
S-28
,

\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)\asymp\frac{{\phi}_{\zeta}(x)}{x^{\zeta-1}},\quad\text{for}\quad x\geq 1.

Noting that
{\phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})=L_{\zeta}^{-1}s_{n}/n
, we see that
(n/s_{n})\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})\asymp(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{-(\zeta-1)}\to 0
. It remains to lower bound
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta_{n})
. For any
\delta>0
, we have

\frac{n}{s_{n}}\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)\asymp\frac{{\phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)}{(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)^{\zeta-1}{\phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})},

provided that
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta\geq 1
.
Lemma
S-29
tells us that for
\delta\geq 0
we have (uniformly in
\delta<1
)

{\phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)/{\phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})\geq\exp\big(\delta\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta\rvert^{\zeta-1}\sign(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)\big).

Thus (uniformly in
\delta<1
),

\frac{n}{s_{n}}\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)\gtrsim(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta)^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp(\delta\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}-\delta\rvert^{\zeta-1})\geq(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp(\delta(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}/2)^{\zeta-1}),

the inequality holding since
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}/2>\delta
for
n
large because
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}\to\infty
. If a sequence
\delta=\delta_{n}\in(0,1)
satisfies
\delta_{n}\geq(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-\upsilon}
for some
\upsilon\in(0,1-1/\zeta)
then the last expression is lower bounded by, for a constant
c=c(\zeta)
,

(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp\big(c(\log n/s_{n})^{1-1/\zeta-\upsilon}\big)\to\infty.

This shows (
S-4
) and (
16
), (
17
) for the pair
(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta},\delta_{n})
. For the pairs
(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n},\delta_{n}+\kappa_{n})
with
\kappa_{n}\to 0
, we observe that, again using Lemmas
S-28
and
S-29
,

\frac{n}{s_{n}}\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n})\leq L_{\zeta}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n})^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp(-\kappa_{n}\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}\rvert^{\zeta-1})\lesssim(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{-(\zeta-1)}\exp(-\kappa_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})^{\zeta-1}),

which leads to (
S-5
) (and (
17
) with
a_{n}^{*}=\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}+\kappa_{n}
).

Next we move to verify the monotonicity conditions, first in the Subbotin location model
f_{a}(x)=\phi_{\zeta}(x-a)
,
x,a\in\mathbb{R}
. To verify (
18
), that is, that
f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x)=\exp(\zeta^{-1}[\lvert x\rvert^{\zeta}-\lvert x-a\rvert^{\zeta}])
is increasing in
x
for
a>0
, it suffices to differentiate the map
x\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\lvert x\rvert^{\zeta}-\lvert x-a\rvert^{\zeta}
on the regions
x\leq 0
,
0\leq x\leq a
and
x\geq a
.

In the Subbotin scale model
f_{a}(x)=\phi_{\zeta}(x/(1+\lvert a\rvert)^{1/2})/(1+\lvert a\rvert)^{1/2}
,
x,a\in\mathbb{R}
. For (
19
), we have
f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x)=\exp(\zeta^{-1}\lvert x\rvert^{\zeta}(1-(1+|a|)^{-\zeta/2}))/(1+|a|)^{1/2}
which is increasing in
x\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma S-2
.

Under Assumption
1
1
,
\overline{F}_{a}(x)=F_{-a}(-x)
for all
x,a\in\mathbb{R}
. Under Assumption
1
2
,
\overline{F}_{a}(x)=F_{a}(-x)
for all
x,a\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Immediate from the conditions on
f_{a}
by substituting in the integrals defining
F_{a}
.
∎

Lemma S-3
.

Under Assumption
1
, for all
a\in\mathbb{R}
(Assumption
1
1
) or
a>0
(Assumption
1
2
), we have that

t\in[0,\infty)\mapsto\overline{F}_{a}(t)/\overline{F}_{0}(t)
is continuous increasing.

Proof.

First observe that by (
18
) or (
19
), we have
\overline{F}_{a}(t)/\overline{F}_{0}(t)>f_{a}(t)/f_{0}(t)
for all
t\geq 0
. Then the proof follows from a simple derivative computation.
∎

S-1.2
Interpretation of Assumption
1

The conditions (
16
) and (
17
) of Assumption
1
can be thought of as light tail conditions. The following shows that Laplace tails are too heavy. In view of the proof, notice that the conditions roughly reduce to requiring the conditional probability

P_{X\sim f_{0}}(\lvert X\rvert>a_{n}^{*}\mid\lvert X\rvert>a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})
to tend to zero for some
a_{n}^{*}\to\infty
and for
\delta_{n}
tending to zero slowly enough.

Lemma S-4
.

Consider the Laplace tail function
\Phi_{\zeta}
,
\zeta=1
, defined as in Example
2
. There do not exist positive numbers
a_{n}^{*}\to\infty
and
\delta_{n}\to 0
such that
(n/s_{n})\overline{\Phi}_{1}(a_{n}^{*})\to 0,\penalty\ (n/s_{n})\overline{\Phi}_{1}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\to\infty
.

Proof.

For any
a_{n}^{*}
and
\delta_{n}
with
a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}\geq 0
we have by memoryless of the exponential distribution (or direct computation)

\overline{\Phi}_{1}(a_{n}^{*})=\overline{\Phi}_{1}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})e^{-\delta_{n}}.

The result follows since
e^{-\delta_{n}}\to 1
if
\delta_{n}\to 0
.
∎

Not only does the assumption not hold for Laplace noise, but also the conclusions are not true. In particular, it is relatively straightforward to show, using similar memorylessness arguments, that the optimal thresholding procedure will have
\FDR
and
\FNR
of the same order, in contrast to the conclusion of Theorem
6
which said that the
\FDR
always contributes negligibly to the combined risk at the boundary.

Finally, note that a related assumption was considered in
[
41
]
, as we discuss in Section
S-13
.

S-2
Sparsity preserving procedures

Here we show that a large class of procedures are sparsity preserving in the sense of Definition
1
, namely: the oracle thresholding procedure, BH procedures with either fixed or vanishing level, and empirical Bayes
\ell
-value procedures with fixed level.

To start with, let us check the sparsity preserving property for the oracle thresholding procedure
\varphi_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*}\}
. Its number of true positives is at most
s_{n}
, and if
V
denotes its number of its false positives, we have
E_{\theta}V\leq(n-s_{n})2\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*})
so Markov’s inequality combined with condition (
17
) give that it is sparsity preserving up to a constant multiplicative factor
A
(e.g.
A=1+\varepsilon
, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
).

The next lemma is useful for procedures for which a control of the FDR is already known.

Lemma S-5
.

For any
\Theta\subset\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
and any multiple testing procedure
\varphi
, we have for any
u>1
and
\theta\in\Theta
,

P_{\theta}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)>us_{n}\right]\leq P_{\theta}\left[\FDP(\theta,\varphi)>1-u^{-1},\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)\geq s_{n}\right]\leq\frac{u}{u-1}\FDR(\theta,\varphi).

(S-6)

In particular, we have

(i)

for any constant
c>1
and any sequence
A=(A_{n})_{n}
for which
A_{n}\geq c
, any sequence of procedures
\varphi
with vanishing FDR in the sense of

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\FDR(\theta,\varphi)=o(1)

is sparsity preserving up to the multiplicative factor
A=(A_{n})_{n}
over
\Theta
, that is,
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Theta)
with Definition
1
.

(ii)

for any sequence
A=(A_{n})_{n}
for which
A_{n}\to\infty
, any sequence of procedures
\varphi
with an FDP bounded uniformly in probability away from
1
when at least
s_{n}
rejections are made, in the sense that for some fixed
t<1
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}P_{\theta}\left(\FDP(\theta,\varphi)>t,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)\geq s_{n}\right)=o(1)

is sparsity preserving up to the multiplicative factor
A=(A_{n})_{n}
over
\Theta
, that is,
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Theta)
with Definition
1
.

An easy consequence of Lemma
S-5
(i) is that the BH procedure taken at a level
\alpha_{n}\to 0
(see Section
S-7
for a formal definition) satisfies the sparsity preserving condition on
\Theta=\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
for any sequence
A=(A_{n})_{n}
with
A_{n}\geq c>1
for all
n
(recall that the FDR of this procedure is at most
\alpha_{n}
, see (
S-19
)). Using Theorem 1 of
[
19
]
, the
\ell
-value procedure also has a vanishing FDR (under polynomial sparsity), so that Lemma
S-5
(i) also ensures that the
\ell
-value procedure is sparsity preserving on
\Theta=\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
. We directly obtain in Section
S-12
sparsity-preservingness of the
\ell
-value procedure on
\Theta=\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
without requiring polynomial sparsity, see Remark
S-11
. Finally Corollary
S-1
below uses Lemma
S-5
(ii) to prove that the BH procedure at a fixed level
\alpha<1
is sparsity preserving.

Proof.

Letting
V=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\varphi_{i}(X)
, we have for all
u>1
,

\displaystyle\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)>us_{n}\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\left\{V>(u-1)s_{n}\right\}\cap\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)\geq s_{n}\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\left\{\frac{V}{V+s_{n}}>\frac{(u-1)s_{n}}{(u-1)s_{n}+s_{n}}\right\}\cap\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)\geq s_{n}\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\left\{\FDP(\theta,\varphi)>1-u^{-1}\right\}\cap\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)\geq s_{n}\right\},

because the function
x\in[0,\infty)\mapsto\frac{x}{x+s_{n}}
is non-decreasing. We conclude the proof of (
S-6
) by taking probabilities and using Markov’s inequality.
Parts
(i)
and
(ii)
are immediate consequences.
∎

Corollary S-1
.

The BH procedure taken at a fixed level
\alpha<1
is sparsity preserving up to any multiplicative factor
A=(A_{n})_{n}
with
A_{n}\to\infty
over any parameter set
\Theta\subset\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
.

Proof.

From (
S-18
), letting
\hat{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi^{BH}_{i}
, classically one deduces that
2\overline{F}_{0}(\hat{t})=\alpha\hat{k}/n
. Thus, for all
\epsilon\in(0,1-\alpha)
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left(\FDP(\theta,\varphi^{BH})>\alpha+\epsilon,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}^{BH}\geq s_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{2\overline{F}_{0}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert)\leq\alpha\hat{k}/n\}>(\alpha+\epsilon)\hat{k},\hat{k}\geq s_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq s_{n}}P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{2\overline{F}_{0}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert)\leq\alpha k/n\}>(\alpha+\epsilon)k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq s_{n}}P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-s_{n}}\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq\alpha k/n\}>(\alpha+\epsilon)k\right)\leq\sum_{k\geq s_{n}}P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq\alpha k/n\}>(\alpha+\epsilon)k\right),

for
U_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, i.i.d. variables uniformly distributed on
(0,1)
. Now, we have by Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
), for any
k\geq s_{n}
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq\alpha k/n\}>(\alpha+\epsilon)k\right)

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq\alpha k/n\}-\alpha k>\epsilon k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-0.5\frac{(\epsilon k)^{2}}{\alpha k+\epsilon k/3}\right)\leq\exp(-c\epsilon^{2}k),

for some universal constant
c>0
(which can be chosen independent of
\alpha
by bounding
\alpha
by
1
).
It follows that

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left(\FDP(\theta,\varphi^{BH})>\alpha+\epsilon,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}^{BH}\geq s_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq s_{n}}\exp(-c\epsilon^{2}k)\leq(1-e^{-c\epsilon^{2}})^{-1}e^{-c\epsilon^{2}s_{n}}=o(1).

The result follows from Lemma
S-5
(ii).
∎

Let us now give an example of a (randomized) non-sparsity-preserving procedure for which some FDR/FNR tradeoff is possible.

Example S-7
.

Given a real
b
and for
\varphi^{*}
a procedure satisfying (
13
), define a (randomized) procedure
\tilde{\varphi}
as follows: given a Bernoulli variable
Z
with success probability
\pi\in[0,1]
, let

\tilde{\varphi}=\tilde{\varphi}(X,Z)=(1-Z)\varphi^{*}(X)+Z.

With probability
\pi
, the test
\tilde{\varphi}
equals the trivial test
1
, so rejects all null hypotheses. Then

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\FDR(\theta,\tilde{\varphi})=\pi\frac{n-s_{n}}{n}+(1-\pi)\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\FDR(\theta,\varphi^{*})=\pi(1+o(1)),

while the FNR is controlled at level

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\FNR(\theta,\tilde{\varphi})=\pi\cdot 0+(1-\pi)\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\FNR(\theta,\varphi^{*})=(1-\pi)\overline{\Phi}(b)(1+o(1)).

In particular, for
\pi>0
, the procedure
\tilde{\varphi}

trades a gain of
\pi\overline{\Phi}(b)
in terms of the FNR with a loss of
\pi
in terms of the FDR. The procedure
\varphi^{*}
is not sparsity preserving, as it makes
n\gg s_{n}
rejections with probability
\pi>0
.

S-3
Link between conservative testing and almost full recovery

We state a lemma to illustrate the link between conservative testing and almost full recovery.

Lemma S-6
.

If a test
\varphi
achieves almost full recovery with respect to the Hamming loss over a subset
\Theta_{s_{n}}\subset\ell_{0}[s_{n}]\backslash\ell_{0}[s_{n}-1]
, that is,
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{s_{n}}}E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}=o(1)
, then it also allows for conservative testing over
\Theta_{s_{n}}
, that is,
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{s_{n}}}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\to 0
.

Conversely, suppose
\varphi
allows for conservative testing over
\Theta_{s_{n}}
, and that the number of false discoveries
V(\varphi)=\sum_{i:\theta_{i}=0}\varphi_{i}
of
\varphi
concentrates around its mean in that,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{s_{n}}}P_{\theta}[\lvert V(\varphi)-E_{\theta}V(\varphi)\rvert>E_{\theta}V(\varphi)/2]=o(1).

(S-7)

Then
\varphi
achieves almost full recovery with respect to the Hamming loss over
\Theta_{s_{n}}
.

Proof of Lemma
S-6
.

Recall that the FDP of
\varphi
can be written
V/\{\max(V+S,1)\}
, where
V=V(\varphi)
is the number of false discoveries of
\varphi
and
S=S(\varphi)
the number of true discoveries.

First suppose
\varphi
achieves almost full recovery with respect to the Hamming loss. This can be written
E[V]+E[s_{n}-S]=o(s_{n})
uniformly over
\Theta
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{V+S>s_{n}/2\}
, then using Markov’s inequality,

P_{\theta}[\mathcal{A}^{c}]\leq P_{\theta}[s_{n}-S\geq s_{n}/2]\leq\frac{2}{s_{n}}E_{\theta}[s_{n}-S]

so that
P_{\theta}[\mathcal{A}^{c}]=o(1)
. On the other hand,

\FDR(\theta,\varphi)=E_{\theta}\left[\frac{V}{(V+S)\vee 1}\right]\leq\frac{2}{s_{n}}E_{\theta}\left[V\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\right]+P_{\theta}[\mathcal{A}^{c}],

which implies FDR
(\theta,\varphi)=o(1)
over
\Theta
, while FNR
(\theta,\varphi)=o(1)
follows from
E_{\theta}[s_{n}-S]=o(s_{n})
.

Conversely, suppose
\varphi
allows for conservative testing over
\Theta
. Then
FNR
(\theta,\varphi)=o(1)
implies
E_{\theta}[s_{n}-S]=o(s_{n})
. Set
\mathcal{B}=\{|V(\varphi)-E_{\theta}V(\varphi)|\leq E_{\theta}V(\varphi)/2\}
. By assumption
P_{\theta}[\mathcal{B}^{c}]=o(1)
over
\Theta
. Then

\FDR(\theta,\varphi)=E_{\theta}\left[\frac{V}{(V+S)\vee 1}\right]\geq\frac{E_{\theta}V/2}{3E_{\theta}V/2+s_{n}}P_{\theta}[\mathcal{B}]=\psi(E_{\theta}V/s_{n})(1+o(1)),

where
\psi
is the bijective continuous map
x\to x/(3x+2)
from
(0,\infty)
to
(0,1/3)
. By assumption
\FDR(\theta,\varphi)=o(1)
. As
\psi^{-1}
is continuous at
0
with limit
0
, one gets
EV_{\theta}/s_{n}=o(1)
which implies combined with the control of
E[s_{n}-S]
that
\varphi
achieves almost full recovery with respect to the Hamming loss.
∎

S-4
Pointwise and local minimax results

Here we give the claimed “pointwise” version of Theorem
4
. Uniformity will be demanded only over permutations,

\theta_{\sigma}=(\theta_{\sigma(i)}:i\leq n).

(S-8)

In particular, we allow the possibility of procedures knowing the exact nonzero values of the
\theta_{i}
, with only their locations unknown.
Recall from (
23
) the definition

\Lambda_{n}(\theta)=s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*}).

Theorem S-1
.

Consider the sparse sequence model (
1
)–(
3
) and grant Assumption
1
1
. The
\mathfrak{R}
-risk satisfies

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\sigma}\mathfrak{R}(\theta_{\sigma},\varphi)=\Lambda_{n}(\theta)+o(1),

where the supremum is over all permutations
\sigma
and the infimum over all testing procedures
\varphi
.
The test
\varphi
may be chosen independently of
\theta
. Under the assumption that
\Lambda_{n}(\theta)>c
for some
c>0
, the remainder
\lvert\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\sigma}\mathfrak{R}(\theta_{\sigma},\varphi)-\Lambda_{n}(\theta)\rvert
is
o(1)

uniformly
in
\theta
.

Under Assumption
1
2
, the same conclusion holds with
2\Lambda_{n}(\theta)-1
in place of
\Lambda_{n}(\theta)
.

Note that we do not make the
o(1)
terms explicit here: this will be investigated in detail in Section
S-9
for certain classes of large signals.

Remark S-8
.

This gives an alternative proof of the first part of Theorem
4
(for
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
bounded away from zero): one applies the above result, noting simply that
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
is closed under permutations and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\Lambda_{n}(\theta)=\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
in the setting of Theorem
4
.

Remark S-9
.

Since the BH procedure
\varphi_{\alpha}^{BH}
is invariant under permutations (
\varphi_{\alpha,\sigma(i)}^{BH}(X_{\sigma})=\varphi_{\alpha,i}(X)
) we deduce that the risk of the BH procedure at some
\theta
is lower bounded by
\Lambda_{n}(\theta)+o(1)
. The upper bound in Theorem
5
is therefore an
equality
, up to
o(1)
terms.

Remark S-10
.

The proof of Theorem
S-1
does not require the full strength of Assumption
1
. In particular, if we omit the assumption that
\overline{F}_{a}(x)
is increasing in
a
from Assumption
1
2
, and replace the assumption
f_{a}(x)=f_{a}(-x)
and
f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x)
is increasing in
x>0
with the single assumption that
f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x)
increases in
\lvert x\rvert
, the pointwise result still holds. However, these extra assumptions remain necessary for Theorem
4
, since they ensure that the “hardest”
\theta
lies on the boundary of
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
.

In contrast, we
do
make full use of Assumption
1
1
in the proof, but if one were to perform one-tailed testing instead (i.e.
H_{1,i}:\theta_{i}>0
), then both the symmetry assumption and the monotonicity of
\overline{F}_{a}(x)
in
a
are no longer needed. Proofs are almost identical, with thresholding procedures replacing absolute value thresholding procedures.

Theorem
6
also has a “pointwise” version.

Theorem S-2
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
S-1
. For any sequence
B=(B_{n})_{n}
with
B_{n}^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{3}(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})
and
\varliminf_{n}B_{n}>1
, and for
\mathcal{S}_{B}(\mathcal{S}_{\theta})
as in Definition
1
the set of sparsity preserving procedures over the set
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}=\{\theta_{\sigma}:\sigma\text{ a permutation}\}
,

\inf_{\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{B}(\mathcal{S}_{\theta})}\sup_{\sigma}\,\FNR(\theta_{\sigma},\varphi)=\Lambda_{n}(\theta)+o(1).

Note that
\mathcal{S}_{B}(\mathcal{S}_{\theta})\supseteq\mathcal{S}_{B}(\Theta)
if
\Theta\supseteq\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
, so that this result strengthens the conclusion of Theorem
6
.

We omit the proof; it is similar to the proof of Theorem
S-1
.
This result in particular implies that asymptotically, the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk of the BH
(\alpha)
procedure for fixed
\alpha>0
must be suboptimal at
any
signal
\theta
, since it must incur an FNR of at least
\Lambda_{n}(\theta)
(using invariance by permutation as above to get the result for a specific
\theta
from the above display) and therefore an
\mathfrak{R}
–risk of at least
\Lambda_{n}(\theta)+\alpha>\Lambda_{n}(\theta)
.

Proof of Theorem
S-1
.

The proof is virtually identical to that of Theorem
4
, particularly in the case of granting Assumption
1
2
. We outline the adjustments required under Assumption
1
1
.

For the upper bound, no change is required, other than skipping the step where we bound
\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})\geq\overline{F}_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})
.

For the lower bound, one follows the proof of Theorem
4
almost exactly, simply noting, with
a_{j}
taking the nonzero values of
\theta_{i}
, that the prior chosen therein gives mass 1 to the set
\{\theta_{\sigma}:\sigma\text{ a permutation}\}
. The only step requiring care is that previously we took
a_{j}>0
for all
j
, and now some
\theta_{i}
may take negative values. This is easily accommodated: in bounding
\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(\varepsilon_{k},a_{j})>h(X_{i},a_{j})\}\geq\rho\right]
we now split the sum into
\{j:a_{j}>0\}
and
\{j:a_{j}<0\}
. For the former we argue as before that
P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(\varepsilon_{k},a_{j})>h(X_{i},a_{j})\}\geq\rho\right]=F_{\lvert a_{j}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1)
by Lemma
S-7
. For the latter, we achieve the same bound by noting that the distribution of
X_{i}
under
f_{a}
is the same as the distribution of
-X_{i}
under
f_{-a}
(and the distribution of
\varepsilon_{k}\sim f_{0}
is symmetric), so that using
h(x,a):=f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x)=h(-x,-a)
we have

\displaystyle P_{X_{i}\sim f_{a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(\varepsilon_{k},a_{j})>h(X_{i},a_{j})\}\geq\rho\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{X_{i}\sim f_{-a_{j}}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(-\varepsilon_{k},a_{j})>h(-X_{i},a_{j})\}\geq\rho\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{X_{i}\sim f_{\lvert a_{j}\rvert}}\left[\#\{k\in Q_{j}\backslash\{i\}\>:\>h(\varepsilon_{k},\lvert a_{j}\rvert)>h(X_{i},\lvert a_{j}\rvert)\}\geq\rho\right],

which is equal to
F_{\lvert a_{j}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1)
by the previous case.

∎

S-5
General lower bounds

We present in this section general lower bounds that can be applied in any model where we observe
X\sim P_{\theta}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For any prior
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote by
P_{\pi}
the distribution of
(X,\theta)
in the Bayesian model where
\theta\sim\pi
and
X\,|\,\theta\sim P_{\theta}
.

The first result is as follows.

Theorem S-3
.

For any prior
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
let
\ell_{i}(X)=P_{\pi}(\theta_{i}=0\>|\>X)
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Then for all
\rho>0
,
s_{n}\geq 1
, and all measurable
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the minimax risk
\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)
is lower bounded by the two following quantities:

(i)

For any
\eta\in(0,1)
,

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\left(\lambda\wedge\frac{\rho\lambda}{1+\rho\lambda}\right)(1-e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}})-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})-2P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta),

for some universal constant
c>0
, where
\lambda=\frac{M_{\rho}(1-\eta)}{s_{n}}
and

M_{\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)].

(ii)

For
m_{\pi,\rho}=P_{\pi}(\exists i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\>:\>\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho))
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\left(\frac{\rho}{s_{n}+\rho}\wedge\frac{1}{s_{n}}\right)m_{\pi,\rho}-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})-2P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta).

The lower bound (i) says roughly that the Bayes risk for
\mathfrak{R}
is lower bounded by the type-two error of the Bayes procedure for the
\rho
-weighted classification risk problem. Note that the bounds above are true for any prior on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Nevertheless, we should choose the sparsity and the signal strength wisely to both make
M_{\rho}
large and make
P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})
small in the lower bound.

Proof.

For all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varphi
, writing
D_{n}(X)=\sum_{i\leq n}\varphi_{i}(X)
and let

Q(\theta,\varphi,X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\frac{\varphi_{i}(X)}{1\vee D_{n}(X)}+\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\frac{1-\varphi_{i}(X)}{1\vee\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}}\right\}.

so that
\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)=E_{\theta}Q(\theta,\varphi,X)
. Classically, since
Q(\theta,\varphi,X)\leq 2
, we have

\mathfrak{R}_{\pi}^{*}:=E_{\pi}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)+2P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta).

Hence, we only have to prove a lower bound for
\mathfrak{R}_{\pi}
.
For this, let

L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\varphi_{i}(X)+\rho\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}(1-\varphi_{i}(X))\right\}.

(S-9)

For any
\delta_{n}>0
, whenever
D_{n}(X)\leq s_{n}(1+\delta_{n})
and
\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}
, we have that
Q(\theta,\varphi,X)
is at least

\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)}{s_{n}}

Also, if
D_{n}(X)\geq s_{n}(1+\delta_{n})
(hence
D_{n}(X)\geq 1
) and
\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}
, we have

Q(\theta,\varphi,X)\geq\frac{D_{n}(X)-\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}}{D_{n}(X)}\geq\frac{D_{n}(X)-s_{n}}{D_{n}(X)}\geq\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}.

Hence, we have for all
\delta_{n}>0
,

\displaystyle E_{\pi}[Q(\theta,\varphi,X)]=E_{\pi}[E_{\pi}[Q(\theta,\varphi,X)\>|\>X]]

\displaystyle\geq E_{\pi}\left(\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)\leq s_{n}(1+\delta_{n})\}\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}\}\>|\>X]}{s_{n}}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)>s_{n}(1+\delta_{n})\}\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}E_{\pi}[\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}\}\>|\>X]\right).

Since

\displaystyle E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}\}\>|\>X]

\displaystyle=E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\>|\>X]-E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n}\}\>|\>X]

\displaystyle\geq E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\>|\>X]-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}[\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n}\>|\>X],

we have almost surely in
X
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)\leq s_{n}(1+\delta_{n})\}\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}\}\>|\>X]}{s_{n}}

\displaystyle+\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)>s_{n}(1+\delta_{n})\}\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}E_{\pi}[\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}\}\>|\>X]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\left\{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{\inf_{\varphi}E_{\pi}(L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)|X)}{s_{n}}\right\}-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}[\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n}\>|\>X].

By integration, this gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\pi}^{*}

\displaystyle\geq E_{\pi}\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\left\{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{\inf_{\varphi}E_{\pi}(L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)|X)}{s_{n}}\right\}\right)-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})

Now solving the Bayes problem for the (weighted) classification loss, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\varphi}E_{\pi}(L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)|X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\ell_{i}(X)\mathbf{1}\{\ell_{i}(X)\leq\rho/(1+\rho)\}+\rho(1-\ell_{i}(X))\mathbf{1}\{\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)\}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\rho L^{\prime}_{\rho},

(S-10)

by letting

L^{\prime}_{\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\ell_{i}(X))\mathbf{1}\{\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)\}.

(S-11)

This entails

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\pi}^{*}

\displaystyle\geq E_{\pi}\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\left\{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{\rho L^{\prime}_{\rho}}{s_{n}}\right\}\right)-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})

(S-12)

For proving (i), we observe that the right-hand side of (
S-12
) is at least

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\left\{\left(\frac{\rho}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge 1\right)\frac{M_{\rho}(1-\eta)}{s_{n}}\right\}(1-e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}})-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n}),

by recalling
M_{\rho}=E_{\pi}L^{\prime}_{\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{\pi}[(1-\ell_{i}(X))\mathbf{1}\{\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)\}]
and because by Bernstein’s inequality,

P_{\pi}\left(L^{\prime}_{\rho}<M_{\rho}(1-\eta)\right)\leq e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}},

for
c>0
some constant. The result (i) then follows by letting
\delta_{n}=\rho M_{\rho}(1-\eta)/s_{n}
.
To prove (ii), we observe that the right-hand side of (
S-12
) is at least

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\left\{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{\rho}{s_{n}}\right\}\right)P_{\pi}(L^{\prime}_{\rho}\geq 1)-n(1\vee\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})

The result (ii) follows by letting
\delta_{n}=\rho/s_{n}
.

∎

The following lower bound is similar to the one of Theorem
S-3
(i), but for the weighted risk

\mathfrak{R}_{W}(\theta,\varphi)=W.\FDR(\theta,\varphi)+\FNR(\theta,\varphi),

(S-13)

where
W>0
is some weight.
In addition, while it is true for any procedure, it is particularly suitable for sparsity preserving procedures (see Definition
1
).

Theorem S-4
.

For any prior
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\ell_{i}(X)=P_{\pi}(\theta_{i}=0\>|\>X)
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Then for all
\rho,W,B>0
,
s_{n}\geq 1
, all measurable
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and all multiple testing procedures
\varphi
, we have for any
\eta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathfrak{R}_{W}(\theta,\varphi)\geq

\displaystyle\left(1\wedge\frac{\rho W}{B}\right)\left(1\wedge\frac{M_{\rho}(1-\eta)}{s_{n}}\right)(1-e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}})-\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}P_{\theta}\left(D_{n}(X)>Bs_{n}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{W}{B}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)(B+\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})-(W+2)P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta),

for some universal constant
c>0
, where
\mathfrak{R}_{W}
denotes the weighted risk (
S-13
) and where

M_{\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)].

Proof.

We use the same method as in the proof of Theorem
S-3
(i), although we provide the full proof here for the sake of completeness.
For all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varphi
, writing
D_{n}(X)=\sum_{i\leq n}\varphi_{i}(X)
and let

Q_{W}(\theta,\varphi,X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}W\frac{\varphi_{i}(X)}{1\vee D_{n}(X)}+\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\frac{1-\varphi_{i}(X)}{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}}\right\}.

so that
\mathfrak{R}_{W}(\theta,\varphi)=E_{\theta}Q_{W}(\theta,\varphi,X)
. Classically, since
Q_{W}(\theta,\varphi,X)\leq W+1
, we have

E_{\pi}\mathfrak{R}_{W}(\theta,\varphi)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathfrak{R}_{W}(\theta,\varphi)+(W+1)P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta).

Hence, we only have to prove

\displaystyle E_{\pi}\mathfrak{R}_{W}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1\wedge\frac{\rho W}{B}\right)\left(1\wedge\frac{M_{\rho}(1-\eta)}{s_{n}}\right)(1-e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}})

\displaystyle-\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}P_{\theta}\left(D_{n}(X)>Bs_{n}\right)-P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta)-\left(\frac{W}{B}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)(B+\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n}).

(S-14)

Recall
L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)
in (
S-9
).
Whenever
D_{n}(X)\leq Bs_{n}
and
\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}
, we have that
Q_{W}(\theta,\varphi,X)
is at least

\left(\frac{W}{B}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)}{s_{n}}

Hence, we have

\displaystyle E_{\pi}[Q_{W}(\theta,\varphi,X)]\geq E_{\pi}[E_{\pi}[Q_{W}(\theta,\varphi,X)\>|\>X]]

\displaystyle\geq E_{\pi}\left(\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)\leq Bs_{n}\}\left(\frac{W}{B}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)\frac{E_{\pi}[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\mathbf{1}\{\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}\leq s_{n}\}\>|\>X]}{s_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\rho W}{B}\wedge 1\right)E_{\pi}\left(\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)\leq Bs_{n}\}\frac{L^{\prime}_{\rho}}{s_{n}}\right)-\left(\frac{W}{B}\wedge\rho^{-1}\right)(B+\rho)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n}),

because
L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}\leq B+\rho
when
D_{n}(X)\leq Bs_{n}

and by using (
S-10
) and defining
L^{\prime}_{\rho}
as in (
S-11
).
Next, we have

\displaystyle E_{\pi}\left(\frac{L^{\prime}_{\rho}}{s_{n}}\mathbf{1}\{D_{n}(X)\leq Bs_{n}\}\right)

\displaystyle\geq E_{\pi}\left(1\wedge\frac{L^{\prime}_{\rho}}{s_{n}}\right)-P_{\pi}\left(D_{n}(X)>Bs_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1\wedge\frac{M_{\rho}(1-\eta)}{s_{n}}\right)P_{\pi}(L^{\prime}_{\rho}\geq M_{\rho}(1-\eta))-P_{\pi}\left(D_{n}(X)>Bs_{n}\right).

We obtain (
S-14
) because

P_{\pi}\left(D_{n}(X)>Bs_{n}\right)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}P_{\theta}\left(D_{n}(X)>Bs_{n}\right)+P_{\pi}\left(\theta\notin\Theta\right)

and

P_{\pi}\left(L^{\prime}_{\rho}<M_{\rho}(1-\eta)\right)\leq e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}},

for
c>0
some constant (as shown in the proof of Theorem
S-3
(i)).
∎

The following lower bound is similar to the one of Theorem
S-3
(i), but somewhat more classical, because it is for the classification risk
E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}
, where
\lc(\theta,\varphi)
is the classification loss given by (
30
).

Theorem S-5
.

For any prior
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
let
\ell_{i}(X)=P_{\pi}(\theta_{i}=0\>|\>X)
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Then for all
s_{n}\geq 1
, and all measurable
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}\geq M/s_{n}-nP_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta),

where
M=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>1/2]
(that is,
M
is
M_{\rho}
of Theorem
S-3
(i) for
\rho=1
).

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the one of Theorem
S-3
(i), with important simplifications. Since
\lc(\theta,\varphi)\leq n
pointwise, we first have for all
\varphi
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}\geq E_{\pi}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}-nP_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta).

Then the result follows using arguments as before because

\inf_{\varphi}E_{\pi}\lc(\theta,\varphi)\geq E_{\pi}\inf_{\varphi}[E_{\pi}[\lc(\theta,\varphi)\>|\>X]]\geq M,

because the Bayes rule is given by
\varphi_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{\ell_{i}(X)>1/2\}
.
∎

The above generic lower bound will be used in combination with the following quantitive result, which is closely related to an observation made in Section
S-1
. There it was noted that
o_{P}(s_{n})
of the nulls exceeded
a_{n}^{*}
in absolute value, while many more than
s_{n}
exceed
a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}
. This formed the basis of the sketch proof that the optimal thresholding procedure thresholds at
a_{n}^{*}
. Here we instead prove that in any collection of roughly
n/s_{n}
nulls, there will be at least
\rho
of these taking absolute values between
a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}
and
a_{n}^{*}
. This is key in proving our lower bound over
all
procedures, using that the classification risk can be decomposed into the sum of risks over blocks (see the proof of Theorem
4
).

Lemma S-7
.

Under Assumption
1
1
, for any integer sequence
\rho=\rho_{n}
satisfying

1\leq\rho\leq(n/2s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})-1

we have

P_{X_{1}\sim f_{a}}(\#\{2\leq i\leq n/s_{n}:\varepsilon_{i}>X_{1}\}\leq\rho-1)=\overline{F}_{a}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1).

In particular, we may choose
\rho
tending to infinity or
\rho=1
. The
o(1)
term is uniform in
a
.

Under Assumption
1
2
, for the same condition on
\rho
we instead have

P_{X_{1}\sim f_{a}}(\#\{2\leq i\leq n/s_{n}:\lvert\varepsilon_{i}\rvert>\lvert X_{1}\rvert\}\leq\rho-1)=2\overline{F}_{a}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1).

Proof.

Under Assumption
1
1
, with
a_{n}^{*},

\delta_{n}
as in the assumption, write
A_{n}
for the event
A_{n}=\big\{\#\{2\leq i\leq n/s_{n}:\varepsilon_{i}>X_{1}\sim f_{a}\}\leq\rho-1\big\}
and set
U_{n}=\#\{2\leq i\leq n/s_{n}:\varepsilon_{i}>a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}\}
; note that

U_{n}\sim\operatorname{Bin}(\lfloor n/s_{n}\rfloor-1,\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})).

Define
V_{n}
correspondingly without the
\delta_{n}
.

Observe that

E[U_{n}]\to\infty,

and similarly we deduce

E[V_{n}]=(\lfloor n/s_{n}\rfloor-1)\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*})\to 0.

For any
\rho=\rho_{n}\geq 1
such that
\rho-1<E[U_{n}]/2
(which is true under the specified condition on
\rho
), we may apply Chebyshev’s inequality with the bound
\Var(U_{n})\leq E[U_{n}]
to obtain

P(U_{n}\leq\rho-1)\leq P(\lvert U_{n}-E[U_{n}]\rvert\geq E[U_{n}]-(\rho-1))\leq P\Big(\lvert U_{n}-E[U_{n}]\rvert>\frac{EU_{n}}{2}\Big)\leq\frac{4}{EU_{n}}\to 0.

Similarly, applying Markov’s inequality, we have for any
\rho\geq 1
,

P(V_{n}>\rho-1)=P(V_{n}\geq\rho)\leq\frac{EV_{n}}{\rho}\to 0.

We have thus shown that on an event
B_{n}
of probability tending to 1,

U_{n}\geq\rho
and
V_{n}\leq\rho-1
. In words, on
B_{n}
, at most
\rho-1
of the numbers
(\varepsilon_{i},\penalty\ 2\leq i\leq n/s_{n})
are larger than
a_{n}^{*}
and at least
\rho
of them are larger than
a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}
. It follows that, on
B_{n}
, the event
A_{n}
holds if
X_{1}>a_{n}^{*}
and fails if
X_{1}\leq a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}
. Thus,

\displaystyle P(A_{n})\geq P_{a}(X_{1}>a_{n}^{*})-P(B_{n}^{c}),

\displaystyle P(A_{n}^{c})\geq P_{a}(X_{1}\leq a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})-P(B_{n}^{c}).

Since
F_{a}
is Lipschitz we deduce the result. [Note we eventually apply this result simultaneously for multiple values of
a
, hence the demand that the
F_{a}
be
uniformly
Lipschitz.]

Under Assumption
1
2
we instead define
U_{n}=\#\{2\leq i\leq n/s_{n}:\lvert\varepsilon_{i}\rvert>a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}\}
and similarly for
V_{n}
. The same arguments as before then imply that
U_{n}\geq\rho
and
V_{n}\leq\rho-1
with probability tending to 1, and hence the probability in question is

P(\lvert X_{1}\rvert>a_{n}^{*})+o(1)=2\overline{F}_{a}(a_{n}^{*})+o(1).\qed

S-6
Some remaining proofs

S-6.1
Proof of Theorem
2

Given the lower bound provided by Theorem
1
, Theorem
2
is a consequence of Theorems
S-6
and
5
and Remark
S-7
. Indeed, for the BH procedure, in Theorem
5
one chooses
f_{a}(x)=\phi(x-a)
to be the Gaussian density; by Lemma
S-1
and Remark
S-7
, Assumption
1
1
is satisfied with
a_{n}^{*}=\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+\kappa_{n}
for
\kappa_{n}=\log(1/\alpha_{n})/\sqrt{\log(n/s_{n})}
and under polynomial sparsity (
12
) the condition on
\alpha_{n}
of Theorem
5
is implied by that of Theorem
2
;
choosing
\bm{a}=(\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+b,\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+b,\dots,\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+b)\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{n}}
, the set
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
exactly equals
\Theta_{b}
, and
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})=\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1)
. Note that
\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+b>0
for
n
large enough, so that this choice of
\bm{a}
is permitted.

For the
\ell
-value procedure, given the multilevel bound Theorem
S-6
, the exact same reasoning applies.

S-6.2
Proof of Theorems
3
and
6

Note from Lemma
S-1
(or see Remark
S-7
) that
a_{n}^{*}=\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}
,
\delta_{n}=(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-1/4}
is valid in Assumption
1
in the Gaussian setting of Theorem
3
. One calculates (similarly to (
S-4
))
that
(n/3s_{n})2\overline{\Phi}(\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}-(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-1/4})\geq\exp((\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/4})
for
n
large. It then follows, up to minor technical details as given in the proof of Theorem
1
, that Theorem
3
is implied by Theorem
6
, hence we restrict our attention to proving the latter result.

It follows from computations in Section
8.2
that the oracle procedure
\varphi_{a_{n}^{*}}
considered in the proof of Theorem
4
makes
o_{P}(s_{n})
false discoveries and hence belongs to the class
\mathcal{S}_{B}
(see also Section
S-2
) and that its FNR is controlled at level
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
+o(1), proving that

\inf_{\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{B}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\leq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1).

[Note the trivial procedure
\varphi=0
, also used in the proof for the case
\Lambda_{n}=1+o(1)
, is sparsity preserving.]

To show a corresponding lower bound, the idea is to work with the weighted risk.
More precisely, assume one can show, for a suitable sequence
W_{n}\to 0
, that

\inf_{\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{B}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\left[W_{n}\FDR(\theta,\varphi)+\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\right]\geq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1).

(S-15)

Then by bounding the FDR from above by
1
one gets

W_{n}+\inf_{\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{B}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\geq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1),

which gives the desired lower bound since
W_{n}\to 0
.
To prove (
S-15
), we follow a method similar to the lower bound part of the proof of Theorem
4
(see Section
8.1
), the only difference being that we consider the weighted risk and use the general lower bound of Theorem
S-4
instead of Theorem
S-3
. More precisely, applying Theorem
S-4
with the same prior as in Section
8.1
, with
B=B_{n}
as in the statement of Theorem
6
,
\rho_{n}=\lfloor n/(3s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\rfloor
and
\eta_{n}=s_{n}^{-1/4}
, and noting that
P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>s_{n})=P_{\pi}(\theta\not\in\Theta)=0
by construction and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}P_{\theta}(\#\{i:\varphi_{i}=1\}>B_{n}s_{n})=o(1)
by assumption, we obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{B}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\left[W_{n}\FDR(\theta,\varphi)+\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(1\wedge\frac{\rho W_{n}}{B_{n}}\right)\left(1\wedge\frac{M_{\rho}(1-\eta)}{s_{n}}\right)(1-e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}})+o(1),

for some universal constant
c>0
and where

M_{\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>\rho/(1+\rho)].

As proved in Section
8.1
, we have
M_{\rho}/s_{n}\geq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1)
under Assumption
1
1
and
M_{\rho}/s_{n}\geq 2\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-1+o(1)

under Assumption
1
2
, see (
36
)–(
37
). This implies
e^{-c\eta^{2}M_{\rho}}=o(1)
. By assumption
B_{n}\leq\sqrt{\rho}
, so taking
W_{n}=1/\sqrt{\rho}
leads to (
S-15
).

S-6.3
Proof of Theorem
7

For the lower bound, we use Theorem
S-5
for the prior given in Section
8.1
and
\Theta=\Theta(a_{b},s_{n})
(clearly, this will give the same lower bound over the larger class
\Theta_{b}^{\prime}
). We then have, for
M=M_{1}
,

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(a_{b},s_{n})}E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}\geq M/s_{n},

because
P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta(a_{b},s_{n}))=0
and for
M/s_{n}\geq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1)=\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1)
by using (
36
) for
\rho=1
and in the Gaussian case.

For the upper bound, given
\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})
write
S_{\theta}=\{i:\theta_{i}\neq 0\}
for the support of
\theta
and write
s_{\theta}=\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert
.
We can decompose the classification loss
\lc(\theta,\varphi)
as the sum
V+(s_{\theta}-S)
, where
V=V(\varphi,\theta)=\sum_{i\not\in S_{\theta}}\varphi_{i}
and
S=S(\varphi,\theta)=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\varphi_{i}
.
It suffices to show that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})}E_{\theta}[s_{\theta}-S]

\displaystyle\leq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(s_{n}),

(S-16)

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})}E_{\theta}V

\displaystyle=o(s_{n}).

(S-17)

We begin with (
S-16
). This holds trivially when
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
since
0\leq s_{\theta}-S\leq s_{\theta}\leq s_{n}
for
\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})
, so we restrict to the cases
b\in\mathbb{R}
fixed or
b=b_{n}\to\infty
. It suffices to show, for a sequence
s_{n}^{\prime}
satisfying
1\ll s_{n}^{\prime}\ll s_{n}
, that

\max_{s_{n}^{\prime}\leq s\leq s_{n}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s)}E_{\theta}[s-S]\leq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(s_{n}),

since for
\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s)
with
0\leq s\leq s_{n}^{\prime}
, we have
0\leq s_{\theta}-S\leq s_{\theta}=s\leq s_{n}^{\prime}=o(s_{n})
.
Let
s_{n}^{\prime\prime}
denote a sequence such that

\max_{s_{n}^{\prime}\leq s\leq s_{n}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s)}E_{\theta}[s-S]=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s_{n}^{\prime\prime})}E_{\theta}[s_{n}^{\prime\prime}-S].

We now apply Theorem
2
with
s_{n}^{\prime\prime}
in place of
s_{n}
(note the asymptotics (
3
)
hold). We have

(s_{n}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}E_{\theta}[s_{n}^{\prime\prime}-S]\leq\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\leq\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1)

and we deduce that
E_{\theta}[s_{n}^{\prime\prime}-S]\leq s_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1))\leq s_{n}(\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1))
as required.

For (
S-17
), the proof differs depending on whether
\varphi
is the
\ell
-value procedure or the BH procedure. For the former one notes that the proof of (
S-49
) in Section
S-8.3
holds for
\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})
not just
\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s_{n})
. Taking the supremum yields the claim.
For the latter one uses Lemma
S-9
in Section
S-7.4
and the fact that
\alpha=\alpha_{n}=o(1)
.
An alternative proof of this upper bound in the
\ell
-value case is provided in Remark
S-12
.

S-7
Materials for BH procedure

S-7.1
Definition

Recall that the BH procedure of level
\alpha
is defined as follows (see, e.g.,
[
43
]
):

\displaystyle\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq\hat{t}\})_{1\leq i\leq n};

(S-18)

\displaystyle\hat{t}

\displaystyle=\min\{t\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}\>:\>\hat{G}_{n}(t)\geq 2\overline{F}_{0}(t)/\alpha\};

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq t\}.

S-7.2
Proof of Theorem
5

First, by
[
9
,
10
]
we have any
\theta
,

\displaystyle\FDR(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})=\alpha(n-\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert)/n\leq\alpha,

(S-19)

where
S_{\theta}
denotes the support of
\theta
.
It thus suffices to prove

\FNR(\theta,\varphi_{\alpha}^{BH})\leq\Lambda_{n}(\theta)+e^{-cs_{n}},\quad c=(1-\Lambda_{n}(\theta))^{2}/32.

We first give the proof under Assumption
1
1
.
We rely on the following inequalities: for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(\hat{t}>t)

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}(\hat{G}_{n}(t)<2\overline{F}_{0}(t)/\alpha)=P_{\theta}(\hat{G}_{n}(t)-G_{n}(t)<2\overline{F}_{0}(t)/\alpha-G_{n}(t)),

where using the symmetry (see Lemma
S-2
)

G_{n}(t)=E_{\theta}\hat{G}_{n}(t)=(1-s_{n}/n)2\overline{F}_{0}(t)+n^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\big(\overline{F}_{\theta_{i}}(t)+\overline{F}_{-\theta_{i}}(t)\big).

Let
a_{n}^{*},\delta_{n}
be as in Assumption
1
. Note that
G_{n}(t)\geq(1-s_{n}/n)2\overline{F}_{0}(t)+n^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t)
and observe that, defining
\Psi_{\theta}:t\mapsto s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t)/\overline{F}_{0}(t)
, the restriction of
\Psi_{\theta}
to
[0,\infty)
is a continuous increasing bijection to
[\Psi_{\theta}(0),\infty)
under (
24
) by Lemma
S-8
. Hence, denoting by

t^{*}_{n}
the only point
t>0
(which exists for
n
large by the lemma) such that
(1-s_{n}/n)2\overline{F}_{0}(t)+(s_{n}/n)s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\theta_{i}}(t)=3\overline{F}_{0}(t)/\alpha
,
we obtain

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(\hat{t}>t^{*}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}(\hat{G}_{n}(t^{*}_{n})-G_{n}(t^{*}_{n})<-\overline{F}_{0}(t^{*}_{n})/\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-0.5\frac{n^{2}\overline{F}_{0}^{2}(t^{*}_{n})/\alpha^{2}}{nG_{n}(t^{*}_{n})+(n/3)\overline{F}_{0}(t^{*}_{n})/\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-0.5\frac{n\overline{F}_{0}^{2}(t^{*}_{n})/\alpha^{2}}{s_{n}/n+(2\alpha+1/3)\overline{F}_{0}(t^{*}_{n})/\alpha}\right),

by using Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
) and the fact that
G_{n}(t)\leq 2\overline{F}_{0}(t)+s_{n}/n
for all
t
. Next, Lemma
S-8
further tells us that
a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}\leq t_{n}^{*}\leq a_{n}^{*}
for
n
large, and
\overline{F}_{0}(t_{n}^{*})\asymp\alpha s_{n}/n
(up to a dependence on
\Lambda_{n}
).
The latter implies that, for
c>0
,

P_{\theta}(\hat{t}>t_{n}^{*})\leq e^{-cs_{n}}.

(S-20)

Indeed, inserting the lower bound
\overline{F}_{0}(t_{n}^{*})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\alpha(1-\Lambda_{n})s_{n}/n
in the numerator and denominator, bounding
2\alpha+1/3
by
7/3
, and bounding
1+(7/9)(1-\Lambda_{n})
by
16/9
, we obtain this with
c=(1/32)(1-\Lambda_{n})^{2}
.

Using symmetry (as in Lemma
S-2
) we thus have

\displaystyle\FNR(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq s_{n}^{-1}E_{\theta}\bigg(\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert<t^{*}_{n}\}\bigg)+e^{-cs_{n}}

(S-21)

\displaystyle\leq s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\big(F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t_{n}^{*})-F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(-t_{n}^{*})\big)+e^{-cs_{n}}\leq s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}F_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t_{n}^{*})+e^{-cs_{n}}.

By monotonicity of
{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}
, using that
t_{n}^{*}\leq a_{n}^{*}
, we have

\FNR(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})\leq\Lambda_{n}(\theta)+e^{-cs_{n}},

which concludes the proof of the pointwise bound. For the uniform bound, simply note that for
\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
we have
\Lambda_{n}(\theta)\leq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
to obtain the result in the case where
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
is bounded away from 1. If there is a subsequence
n_{j}
along which
\Lambda_{n_{j}}(\bm{a})\to 1
, the bound (
S-19
) together with
\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\leq 1
for any
\theta,\varphi
yields
\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})\leq 1+\alpha_{n}=\Lambda_{n_{j}}(\bm{a})+o(1)
.

The proof under Assumption
1
2
is identical up to the bound (
S-21
). [It is perhaps worth noting that in this setting is is more natural to define
t_{n}^{*}
relative to
G_{n}(t)=(1-s_{n}/n)2\overline{F}_{0}(t)+n^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}2\overline{F}_{\theta_{i}}(t)
itself rather than relative to the lower bound
(1-s_{n}/n)2\overline{F}_{0}(t)+n^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t)
, but the latter bound remains valid and yields valid deductions.] The first inequality of (
S-21
) holds in this setting, and the result follows from

E_{\theta}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert<t_{n}^{*}\}=1-2\overline{F}_{\theta_{i}}(t_{n}^{*})
, monotonicity of this expression and the fact that
t_{n}^{*}\leq a_{n}^{*}
.

S-7.3
A classification risk bound for BH

Consider the BH procedure
\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha}
(
S-18
), for
\alpha=\alpha_{n}=o(1)
with
-\log\alpha=o((\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})
.
Recalling the definition (
30
) of the classification loss
L_{C}
, let us prove that for any fixed real
b
and any
\eta>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})\geq(\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta)s_{n}\right]=o(1),

(S-22)

and that the same holds with
\overline{\Phi}(b)
replaced by 0 when
b=b_{n}\to+\infty
or by 1 when
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
.
This entails the BH part of Theorem
S-11
(to come in Section
S-12
) under polynomial sparsity (
12
), because

-\log\alpha=o((\log n)^{1/2})
implies

-\log\alpha=o((\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})
in that case.

Fix
\theta\in\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
and
denote by
V=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}^{BH}\neq 0\}
the number of false discoveries of
\varphi^{BH}
and by
W=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}^{BH}=0\}
the number of false non-discoveries of
\varphi^{BH}
.
Assume first
b\in\mathbb{R}
. We have

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})\geq(\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta)s_{n}\right]\leq P_{\theta}\left[V\geq\eta s_{n}/2\right]+P_{\theta}\left[W\geq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta s_{n}/2\right].

We have by Markov’s inequality and Lemma
S-9
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left[V\geq\eta s_{n}/2\right]\leq(\eta/2)^{-1}\frac{E_{\theta}V}{s_{n}}\leq(\eta/2)^{-1}\left(\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{s_{n}(1-\alpha)^{2}}\right),

hence
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}P_{\theta}\left[V\geq\eta s_{n}/2\right]=o(1)
.
On the other hand, we have by using (
S-20
) (note that we use
\Lambda_{n}=\overline{\Phi}(b)<1
here)

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left[W\geq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta s_{n}/2\right]

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert<\hat{t}\}\geq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta s_{n}/2\right]

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|<t_{n}^{*}\}\geq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta s_{n}/2\right]+e^{-cs_{n}},

where
t_{n}^{*}>0
is defined as in Lemma
S-8
. Using also Lemma
S-1
we have in the Gaussian setting that
t_{n}^{*}=a_{n}^{*}+o(1)
with
a_{n}^{*}=\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}
. Using the bound (
S-21
), the random variable
\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert<t_{n}^{*}\}
is stochastically upper-bounded by a Binomial distribution with parameter
s_{n}
and
q_{n}:=1-\overline{\Phi}(t_{n}^{*}-a_{n}^{*}-b)=\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1)
. Applying Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
), we obtain

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert<t_{n}^{*}\}\geq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta s_{n}/2\right]

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}\left[\mathcal{B}(s_{n},q_{n})\geq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta s_{n}/2\right]

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}\left[\mathcal{B}(s_{n},q_{n})-s_{n}q_{n}\geq s_{n}(\overline{\Phi}(b)-q_{n})+\eta s_{n}/2\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-0.5s_{n}\frac{(\overline{\Phi}(b)-q_{n}+\eta/2)^{2}}{q_{n}+(\overline{\Phi}(b)-q_{n}+\eta/2)/3}\right)\leq e^{-c^{\prime}s_{n}},

for
n
large enough and some constant
c^{\prime}>0
only depending on
\eta
(using the bounds
q_{n}\leq 1
and
\overline{\Phi}(b)-q_{n}=o(1)
). This gives (
S-22
) for
b\in\mathbb{R}
.

When
b=b_{n}\to+\infty
(so
\overline{\Phi}(b)\to 0
) and
\eta\in(0,1)
, the same techniques as above lead to

P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})\geq\eta s_{n}\right]\leq(\eta/2)^{-1}\left(\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{s_{n}(1-\alpha)^{2}}\right)+e^{-cs_{n}}+P_{\theta}\left[\mathcal{B}(s_{n},q_{n})\geq\eta s_{n}/2\right],

and this again shows (
S-22
) (with
\overline{\Phi}(b)
replaced by
0
) because

P_{\theta}\left[\mathcal{B}(s_{n},q_{n})\geq\eta s_{n}/2\right]\leq 2q_{n}/\eta=o(1)

by Markov’s inequality.

Finally, when
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
(so
\overline{\Phi}(b)\to 1
), we directly use
W\leq s_{n}
, so that

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha})\geq(1+\eta)s_{n}\right]\leq P_{\theta}\left[V\geq\eta s_{n}\right]\leq\eta^{-1}\left(\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{s_{n}(1-\alpha)^{2}}\right),

by using again Markov’s inequality and Lemma
S-9
. This shows (
S-22
) (with
\overline{\Phi}(b)
replaced by
1
) in that case.

S-7.4
Useful lemmas

Lemma S-8
.

In the setting of Theorem
5
with
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|S_{\theta}|=s_{n}\geq 1
, and
a_{n}^{*},\delta_{n}
as in Assumption
1
, define

\Psi_{\theta}:t\mapsto s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t)/\overline{F}_{0}(t).

Then
\Psi_{\theta}
is continuous increasing on
[0,\infty)
(
n,\theta
being kept fixed), with
1\leq\Psi_{\theta}(0)\leq 2
.
If
\alpha=\alpha_{n}\leq 1
satisfies
3(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*})/(1-\Lambda_{n})\leq\alpha_{n}\leq\min\{1,(n/s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\}
for
n
large enough (that is, (
24
) holds), then, for
n
large enough, there exists a unique point
t^{*}_{n}>0
such that

2(1-s_{n}/n)+(s_{n}/n)\Psi_{\theta}(t_{n}^{*})=3/\alpha,

(S-23)

and we have

a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}\leq t_{n}^{*}\leq a_{n}^{*},

and
\tfrac{1}{3}(1-\Lambda_{n})\alpha_{n}s_{n}/n\leq\overline{F}_{0}(t_{n}^{*})\leq\alpha_{n}s_{n}/n
.

Proof.

The continuous increasing part is a direct consequence of Lemma
S-3
.
For
1\leq\Psi_{\theta}(0)\leq 2
, note that under Assumption
1
necessarily
\overline{F}_{0}(0)=1/2
and
\overline{F}_{0}(0)\leq\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(0)\leq 1
for any
\theta_{i}
(under Assumption
1
2
we in fact have
\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(0)=\overline{F}_{0}(0)=1/2
for all
\theta_{i}
).

Existence of
t_{n}^{*}
will implicitly follow from the proceeding calculations and the intermediate value theorem. To verify the asymptotics of
t_{n}^{*}
, observe that by assumption there exist
c_{n}\to 0,C_{n}\to\infty
such that
\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*})=c_{n}s_{n}/n,

\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})=C_{n}s_{n}/n
.
Then

\Psi_{\theta}(a_{n}^{*})=(n/s_{n})c_{n}^{-1}s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*})=(n/s_{n})c_{n}^{-1}(1-\Lambda_{n}(\theta)).

From the assumed lower bound on
\alpha_{n}
we have that
\alpha_{n}\geq 3c_{n}/(1-\Lambda_{n})
, hence the right side in the above display is at least
(3/\alpha_{n})(n/s_{n})\geq 2+(3/\alpha_{n}-2)(n/s_{n})=\Psi_{\theta}(t_{n}^{*})
, so that
t_{n}^{*}\leq a_{n}^{*}
by monotonicity.
Similarly,

\Psi_{\theta}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})=(n/s_{n})C_{n}^{-1}s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\leq(n/s_{n})C_{n}^{-1},

so that, since
\alpha_{n}\leq\min\{1,C_{n}\}
, we obtain
\Psi_{\theta}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})\leq 2+(3/\alpha_{n}-2)(n/s_{n})=\Psi_{\theta}(t_{n}^{*})
, so that
t_{n}^{*}\geq a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n}
.
Finally, from the definition of
t_{n}^{*}
we have

s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\overline{F}_{\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert}(t_{n}^{*})=(2+(3/\alpha_{n}-2)(n/s_{n}))\overline{F}_{0}(t_{n}^{*}).

The left side is upper bounded by 1 and, using that
t_{n}^{*}\leq a_{n}^{*}
, lower bounded by
1-\Lambda_{n}
; the claimed explicit bounds on
\overline{F}_{0}(t_{n}^{*})
follow using that
(1/\alpha_{n})(n/s_{n})\leq 2+(3/\alpha_{n}-2)(n/s_{n})\leq(3/\alpha_{n})(n/s_{n})
.
∎

Lemma S-9
.

For all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, considering the BH procedure
\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha}
(
S-18
) at some level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then
V=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}^{BH}\neq 0\}
satisfies

E_{\theta}V\leq\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}+\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}.

(S-24)

Proof.

To prove (
S-24
), we follow the proof of Proposition 7.2 in
[
39
]
(or alternatively, that of Lemma 7.1 in
[
11
]
). Recall that we have

\displaystyle E_{\theta}V

\displaystyle=E_{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq\hat{t}\}

with
\hat{t}=\max\{t\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}\>:\>\hat{G}_{n}(t)\geq 2\overline{F}_{0}(t)/\alpha\}
and
\hat{G}_{n}(t)=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq t\}
. Now consider the BH procedure applied to the
X_{i}
’s where we have plugged
X_{i}=+\infty
on
S_{\theta}=\{i\>:\>\theta_{i}\neq 0\}
, that is, with threshold
\hat{t}^{0}=\max\{t\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}\>:\>\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert/n+n^{-1}\sum_{i\notin S_{\theta}}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq t\}\geq 2\overline{F}_{0}(t)/\alpha\}
.
Clearly, we have
\hat{t}^{0}\leq\hat{t}
, so that

\displaystyle E_{\theta}V

\displaystyle\leq E_{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq\hat{t}^{0}\}.

Denote
n_{0}=\lvert S_{\theta}^{c}\rvert
for short.
From classical multiple testing theory (see, e.g., Lemma 7.1 in
[
45
]
), the latter is the expected number of rejections of the step-up procedure with critical values
(\alpha(k+\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert)/n)_{1\leq k\leq n_{0}}
and restricted to the
p
-value set
\{2\overline{F}_{0}(X_{i}),i\notin S_{\theta}\}
. Now from Lemma 4.2 in
[
24
]
(applied with “
n=n_{0}
”, “
\beta=\alpha
” and “
\tau=\alpha/n
”), the latter equals

\displaystyle\alpha\frac{n_{0}}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{0}-1}{n_{0}-1\choose i}(\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert+i+1)i!(\alpha/n)^{i}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\sum_{i\geq 0}(\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert+i+1)\alpha^{i}=\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert+\alpha/(1-\alpha)^{2}.

∎

S-8
An empirical Bayes multiple testing procedure

In this section we define concretely a Bayesian ‘
\ell
-value procedure’ and show that it achieves the minimax risk adaptively, proving (a part of) Theorems
2
and
S-11
. We in fact prove a generalisation of the
\ell
-value part of Theorem
2
to the multiple signals setting of Section
3
, though remaining in the Gaussian sequence setting. Results in this section can be seen as generalisations of Lemmas 5, 7 and 9 in
[
2
]
and corresponding earlier results in
[
19
]
.

S-8.1
Definitions

For
w\in(0,1)
, let
\Pi_{w}=\Pi_{w,\gamma}
denote a spike and slab prior for
\theta
, where, for
\mathcal{G}
a distribution with density
\gamma
,

\Pi_{w}=((1-w)\delta_{0}+w\mathcal{G})^{\otimes n}.

(S-25)

That is, the coordinates of a draw
\theta^{\prime}
from
\Pi_{w}
are independent, and are either exactly equal to
0
, with probability
(1-w)
, or are drawn from the ‘slab’ density
\gamma
. When the Bayesian model holds, the data
X
follows a mixture distribution, with each coordinate
X_{i}
independently having density
(1-w)\phi+wg
, where
g
denotes the convolution
\phi\star\gamma
. [In keeping with the rest of this paper, and in contrast to many papers on empirical Bayesian procedures including
[
2
,
19
]
, we will reserve
\theta
for the “true” parameter under which we analyse the performance of procedures
\varphi
, and so we use the notation
\theta^{\prime}
to denote a draw from the prior/posterior.] We consider a ‘quasi-Cauchy’ alternative as in
[
36
]
, where
\gamma
is defined implicitly such that

g(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}x^{-2}(1-e^{-x^{2}/2}),\>\>\>x\in\mathbb{R}.

(S-26)

The posterior distribution
\Pi_{w}(\cdot\mid X)
can be explicitly derived hence, taking an empirical Bayes approach, one may estimate
\hat{w}
by maximising the log-likelihood

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{w\in[1/n,1]}L(w),

(S-27)

\displaystyle L(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\phi(X_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(1+w\beta(X_{i})),\quad\beta(x):=\tfrac{g}{\phi}(x)-1.

(S-28)

Note that a maximiser can be seen to exist under the current assumptions by taking derivatives (see also Lemma
S-13
).

For an arbitrary level
t\in(0,1)
, we consider the multiple testing procedure given by thresholding the posterior probabilities of coming from the null (also known as ‘
\ell
-values’),

\displaystyle\varphi^{\hat{\ell}}(X)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}\{\ell_{i,\hat{w}}(X)<t\})_{i\leq n},

(S-29)

\displaystyle\ell_{i,w}(X)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{w}(\theta^{\prime}_{i}=0\mid X)=\frac{(1-w)\phi(X_{i})}{(1-w)\phi(X_{i})+wg(X_{i})}.

(S-30)

Let us gather various other definitions as in
[
19
]
and
[
35
]
. Useful properties of these quantities are given in Section
S-8.4
.
We denote
r(w,t)=wt/\{(1-w)(1-t)\}
and set

\displaystyle\beta(x,w)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta(x)}{1+w\beta(x)},\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ w\in(0,1)

(S-31)

\displaystyle\tilde{m}(w)

\displaystyle=-E_{\theta_{1}=0}[\beta(X_{1},w)],\quad w\in(0,1)

(S-32)

\displaystyle m_{1}(\tau,w)

\displaystyle=E_{\theta_{1}=\tau}[\beta(X_{1},w)],\quad\tau\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ w\in(0,1)

(S-33)

\displaystyle m_{2}(\tau,w)

\displaystyle=E_{\theta_{1}=\tau}(\beta(X_{1},w)^{2}),\quad\tau\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ w\in(0,1)

(S-34)

\displaystyle\xi(u)

\displaystyle=(\phi/g)^{-1},\quad u\in(0,(\phi/g)(0))

(S-35)

\displaystyle\zeta(u)

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}(1/u),\quad u\in(0,1).

(S-36)

S-8.2
Main
\ell
-value result

We prove the following. Recall the definition (
21
) of the ‘multiple levels’ signal set
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
. Recall also the definition (
22
) of the measure of the signal in the class
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
, which here evaluates to, with
a_{n}^{*}=\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}
,

\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})=s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}\overline{\Phi}(a_{j}-a_{n}^{*}).

Theorem S-6
.

In the additive Gaussian sparse sequence model (
4
), assume
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
is bounded away from 1. Then the
\ell
-value procedure (
S-29
) taken at any fixed threshold
t\in(0,1)
achieves the risk bound

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})=\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1).

Proof.

The lower bound is given by Theorem
4
, and it remains to prove that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})\leq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+o(1).

Step 1 (concentration of
\hat{w}
)

We construct
w_{-}\leq w_{+}
satisfying
w_{-}\asymp w_{+}\asymp(s_{n}/n)(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2}
such that for
\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
,

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}\not\in(w_{-},w_{+}))=o(1).

For a constant
\nu\in(0,1/2)
we let
w_{-},w_{+}
be the (almost surely unique) solutions to

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})=(1+\nu)(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(w_{-}),

(S-37)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{+})=(1-\nu)(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(w_{+}),

(S-38)

whose existence (for
n
large) and asymptotics are yielded by Lemma
S-10
. By Lemma
S-11
, for a constant
c>0
we have

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}\in(w_{-},w_{+}))\geq 1-e^{-cs_{n}}=1-o(1).

Step 2 (FNR control)

Note that
\ell_{i,w}
monotonically decreases as
w
increases (see Lemma
S-13
), so that on the event
\hat{w}\in(w_{-},w_{+})
we have

\ell_{i,w_{+}}\leq\ell_{i,\hat{w}}\leq\ell_{i,w_{-}}
.

We have (see Lemma
S-19
)

\xi(u)\leq(2\log(1/u)+2\log\log(1/u)+6\log 2)^{1/2}.

The right side is decreasing in
u
hence, using that
w_{-}\geq s_{n}/n
for
n
large enough, we have for any
t\in(0,1)
, a constant
c=c(t)
and a sequence
\eta_{n}=\eta_{n}(t)\to 0

\xi(tw_{-}/2)\leq\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})+2\log\log(n/s_{n})+c}=\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+\eta_{n},

the latter equality coming from Taylor expanding.
We deduce, using that
\xi(u)=(\phi/g)^{-1}(u)
is decreasing (Lemma
S-13
), that if
\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+\eta_{n}
then

\ell_{i,w_{-}}=\Big(1+\frac{w_{-}}{1+w_{-}}\frac{g}{\phi}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert)\Big)^{-1}<\Big(\frac{w_{-}}{2\xi^{-1}(\lvert X_{i}\rvert)}\Big)^{-1}\leq t,

hence, using that
\overline{\Phi}
is Lipschitz, there exists a sequence
\xi_{n}\to 0
not depending on
\theta
such that, with
\varepsilon_{i}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,

P_{\theta}(\ell_{i,w_{-}}\geq t)\leq P(-\varepsilon_{i}\geq\theta_{i}-\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}-\eta_{n})\leq\overline{\Phi}\big(\theta_{i}-\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}\big)+\xi_{n}.

We deduce, writing
S=\#\{i\in S_{\theta}:\ell_{i,\hat{w}}<t\}
and appealing to Lemma
S-25
, that we may upper bound
s_{n}-S
on the event
\hat{w}\geq w_{-}
by a variable
N
following a Poisson binomial distribution with parameter vector
\bm{p}=(\overline{\Phi}(a_{j}-a_{n}^{*})+\xi_{n})_{j\leq s_{n}}
.
For some sequence
\xi_{n}^{\prime}\to 0
we see that the false negative rate is then

\FNR(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})=s_{n}^{-1}E_{\theta}[s_{n}-S]\leq P_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})+s_{n}^{-1}E[N]\leq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+\xi_{n}^{\prime}.

(S-39)

A concentration argument further yields that on an event of probability tending to 1,

S\geq s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a}))/2,

(S-40)

which will be used in the next step.
Indeed observe, using the assumption that
1-\Lambda_{n}
is bounded away from 0, that for
n
large

EN=s_{n}(\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})+\xi_{n})\leq s_{n}\Lambda_{n}+(1/4)s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n}).

Using this bound and Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
) yields

P\big(N>s_{n}\Lambda_{n}+\tfrac{1}{2}s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})\big)\leq P\big(N-EN>\tfrac{1}{4}s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})\big)\to 0,

hence

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\big(S<s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})/2\big)

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}\big(s_{n}-S>s_{n}\Lambda_{n}+s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})/2\big)

\displaystyle\leq P\big(N>s_{n}\Lambda_{n}+\tfrac{1}{2}s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})\big)+P_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})\to 0.

Step 3 (FDR control)

Let
V
denote the number of false positives,

V=\#\{i\not\in S_{\theta}:\ell_{i,\hat{w}}<t\}.

By (
S-40
), let
\mathcal{A}
be an event of probability tending to 1 on which
S\geq s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})/2
and
\hat{w}<w_{+}
. Define

V^{\prime}=\#\{i\not\in S_{\theta}:\ell_{i,w_{+}}<t\}.

Using monotonicity and applying Jensen’s inequality to the convex function
x\mapsto x/(a+x)
we obtain

\FDR(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})=E_{\theta}\Big[\frac{V}{(V+S)\vee 1}\Big]\leq E_{\theta}\Big[\frac{V}{V+S}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\Big]+P_{\theta}(\mathcal{A}^{c})\\
\leq E_{\theta}\Big[\frac{V^{\prime}}{V^{\prime}+s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})/2}\Big]+o(1)\leq\frac{E_{\theta}V^{\prime}}{E_{\theta}V^{\prime}+s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})/2}+o(1).

(S-41)

Next, Lemma
S-14
yields

E_{\theta}V^{\prime}=(n-s_{n})P_{\theta}(\ell_{i,w_{+}}\leq t)\leq 2(n-s_{n})r(w_{+},t)\xi(r(w_{+},t))^{-3},

(S-42)

where
r(w_{+},t)=w_{+}t(1-w_{+})^{-1}(1-t)^{-1}\asymp w_{+}
. We note that
\xi(u)\sim(2\log(1/u))^{1/2}
as
u\to 0
by Lemma
S-19
, so that using
w_{+}\asymp(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}
, we have

E_{\theta}V^{\prime}\lesssim s_{n}\log(n/s_{n})^{-1}=o(s_{n})
.
Since we are assuming
\Lambda_{n}
is bounded away from 1, this yields the bound

\FDR(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})\leq\frac{o(s_{n})}{o(s_{n})+s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n})/2}+o(1)=o(1).

Combined with the bound (
S-39
) on the false negative rate, this concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma S-10
.

In the setting of Theorem
S-6
, for
n
large there exist solutions
w_{-}\leq w_{+}
to (
S-37
) and (
S-38
) respectively. Moreover, these solutions are almost surely unique and satisfy

w_{\pm}\asymp s_{n}(n-s_{n})^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{\pm})^{-1}\asymp s_{n}(n-s_{n})^{-1}(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2}\asymp(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 5]
, adapting to allow for the weaker and mixed signals considered here, and taking advantage of not targeting a rate of convergence to simplify some aspects.
We claim that, for some constants
c,C>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},c(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2})

\displaystyle>(1+\nu)(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(c(s_{n}/n)(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})

(S-43)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}\big(\theta_{i},C(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle<(1-\nu)(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}\big(C(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}\big),

(S-44)

at least for
n
large enough.
It will follow by the intermediate value theorem that there exist unique
w_{-},w_{+}
solving (
S-37
) and (
S-38
) respectively, and satisfying
