(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}\lesssim w_{-}\leq w_{+}\lesssim(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}
, since
\tilde{m}
is continuous, increasing and non-negative and
m_{1}(\tau,\cdot)
is continuous and decreasing for each fixed
\tau
(see Lemma
S-16
).

To prove the claim, note that also by Lemma
S-16
we have, for some
c_{0},C_{0}>0
, any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and asymptotically as
w\to 0
,

\displaystyle c_{0}(\log(1/w))^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{m}(w)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(\log(1/w))^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}m_{1}(\mu,w)

\displaystyle\leq 1/w.

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},C(s_{n}/n)(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq C^{-1}n(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-1/2},

\displaystyle(1-\nu)(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(C(s_{n}/n)(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})

\displaystyle\gtrsim(n-s_{n})(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-1/2},

where the suppressed constant can be chosen independently of
C>0
and
\nu\in(0,1/2)
, for
n
larger than some
N=N(C)
. The inequality (
S-44
) follows, for
C
large enough.

For the lower bound on (
S-43
) we observe that Lemma
S-16
further yields, for some constants
\omega_{0}\in(0,1),M_{0},c_{1}>0
and all
w\leq\omega_{0}
,
\mu\geq M_{0}
,

m_{1}(\mu,w)\geq c_{1}\frac{\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w)-\mu)}{w}T_{\mu}(w),

where
T_{\mu}(w)
is a function bounded below by 1. Recall that
\zeta
is a decreasing function satisfying
\zeta(w)\leq(2\log(1/w)+2\log\log(1/w)+C)^{1/2}
for a constant
C>0
(see Lemmas
S-13
and
S-18
). In particular, note by a Taylor expansion that
\zeta(c(s_{n}/n)(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})\leq\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}+o(1)
and hence, using also that
\overline{\Phi}
is Lipschitz, for some
C_{1}>0
and some
o(1)
not depending on
a_{j}
or
c
we have for
n
large and
a_{j}\geq M_{0}

m_{1}(a_{j},c(s_{n}/n)(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})\geq C_{1}c^{-1}(n/s_{n})(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-1/2}(\overline{\Phi}(\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}-a_{j})-o(1)).

Observe also, recalling the definition (
S-31
), that
\beta(x,w)\geq-\lvert\beta(0)\rvert/(1-\lvert\beta(0)\rvert)
is lower bounded by a constant, hence the same is true of
m_{1}(\tau,w)
for all
\tau
and
w
(including
\tau<M_{0}
).
Let
\sigma
denote a bijection taking
i\in S_{\theta}
to
\sigma(i)=j\in\{1,\dots,s_{n}\}
such that
\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert\geq a_{j}
, and recall that
a_{n}^{*}=\sqrt{2\log(n/s_{n})}
in the current Gaussian setting.
Noting that
\overline{\Phi}(a_{n}^{*}-a_{j})=o(1)
uniformly in
j
such that
a_{j}\leq M_{0}
, we deduce that for some constants
C_{2},c_{2}>0
not depending on
c

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\frac{cs_{n}(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2}}{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}:a_{\sigma(i)}<M_{0}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\frac{cs_{n}(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2}}{n})+\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}:a_{\sigma(i)}\geq M_{0}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\frac{cs_{n}(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})}{n}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-C_{2}s_{n}+\frac{c_{2}n}{cs_{n}(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2}}\sum_{j\leq s_{n}:a_{j}\geq M_{0}}(\overline{\Phi}(a_{n}^{*}-a_{j})-o(1))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-C_{2}s_{n}+\frac{c_{2}n}{cs_{n}(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2}}\big(\sum_{j\leq s_{n}}(\overline{\Phi}(a_{n}^{*}-a_{j})-o(1))\big)

\displaystyle=-C_{2}s_{n}+\frac{c_{2}n}{c(\log n/s_{n})^{1/2}}(1-\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-o(1)).

Note that
s_{n}=o(n)
implies
s_{n}=o(n/(\log(n/s_{n}))^{1/2})
.
Since
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
is bounded away from 1 we deduce that for
n
large the left side of (
S-43
) is lower bounded by a constant not depending on
c
multiplied by
c^{-1}n(\log n/s_{n})^{-1/2}
, while the right side, using the earlier upper bound on
\tilde{m}(w)
, is upper bounded by a constant not depending on
c
multiplied by
n\log(n/s_{n})^{-1/2}
. Taking
c
small enough yields the claim.
∎

Lemma S-11
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
S-10
, define
\hat{w},

w_{-}
and
w_{+}
as in (
S-27
),(
S-37
) and (
S-38
) respectively. Then there exists
c>0
such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\bm{b}}}P_{\theta}\big(\hat{w}\not\in(w_{-},w_{+})\big)\leq e^{-cs_{n}}.

Proof.

We adapt the proof of
[
19
, Lemma S-4]
or
[
2
, Lemma 7]
to the current setting with no polynomial sparsity and multiple signal levels
a_{j}
.

Let us prove, for a constant
c>0
depending only on an upper bound for
\nu<1
, that

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})\leq e^{-cs_{n}}.

The proof that
P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>w_{+})\leq e^{-cs_{n}}
is similar (see also the similar proof below of Lemma
S-12
), yielding the claim up to a factor of 2 which can be removed by initially considering a
c^{\prime}>c
.

Let
S=L^{\prime}
be the score function, that is, the derivative of the likelihood
L
defined in (
S-28
). Since
\hat{w}
maximises
L(w)
, necessarily
S(\hat{w})\leq 0
or
\hat{w}=1
. If
\hat{w}<w_{-}
then only the former may hold, so that applying the strictly monotonic function
S
(Lemma
S-13
) we obtain
\{\hat{w}<w_{-}\}=\{S(w_{-})<S(\hat{w})\}\subseteq\{S(w_{-})<0\}
. Hence,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})\leq P_{\theta}(S(w_{-})<0)

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}(S(w_{-})-E_{\theta}S(w_{-})<-E_{\theta}S(w_{-}))

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}<-E\right),

where we have introduced the notation
W_{i}=\beta(X_{i},w_{-})-m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})
and
E=E_{\theta}S(w_{-})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})
. For
n
large,
\lvert W_{i}\rvert\leq\mathcal{M}:=2/w_{-}
a.s. (see Lemma
S-15
), so that we may apply Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
) and obtain

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})\leq e^{-0.5E^{2}/(V_{2}+\mathcal{M}E/3)},

where
V_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Var(W_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{2}(\theta_{i},w)
, for
m_{2}(\theta_{i},w)=E_{\theta}(\beta(X_{i},w)^{2})
.
In view of the definition (
S-37
) of
w_{-}
, we have

E=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})-(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(w_{-})=\nu(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(w_{-}).

We also note, using the bounds on
m_{2}
in Lemma
S-17
that for some constants
C,M_{0}>0
and
n
larger than some universal threshold,

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\leq n:|\theta_{i}|>M_{0}}m_{2}(\theta_{i},w_{-})+\sum_{i\leq n:\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert\leq M_{0}}m_{2}(\theta_{i},w_{-})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{w_{-}}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}:\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert>M_{0}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})+C\sum_{i\leq n:\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert\leq M_{0}}\frac{\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w_{-})-|\theta_{i}|)}{w_{-}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{w_{-}}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})-\frac{C}{w_{-}}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}:\lvert\theta_{i}\rvert\leq M_{0}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})+Cs_{n}\frac{\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w_{-})-M_{0})}{w_{-}^{2}}+Cn\frac{\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w_{-}))}{w_{-}^{2}},

with
\zeta
defined as in (
S-36
).
For the first term we use the definition (
S-37
) of
w_{-}
, and for the second we use that
m_{1}(\theta_{i},w_{-})
is bounded below by a (negative) constant. By a standard normal tail bound (included in Lemma
S-28
), the bounds on
w_{-}
, the fact that
\sqrt{2\log(1/w_{-})}\leq\zeta(w_{-})\lesssim\sqrt{\log(n/s_{n})}
as
n\to\infty
(Lemmas
S-10
and
S-18
; note
\phi(\zeta(w_{-}))\lesssim w_{-}
as a consequence of the bound) and that
\tilde{m}(w_{-})\asymp\zeta(w_{-})^{-1}
(Lemma
S-16
), we deduce for some constant
M_{1}
that

\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w_{-})-M_{0})\asymp\frac{\phi(\zeta(w_{-})-M_{0})}{\zeta(w_{-})}\lesssim w_{-}\tilde{m}(w_{-})e^{M_{1}\sqrt{\log(n/s_{n})}},

so that, bounding
M_{1}\sqrt{\log(n/s_{n})}
by
\log(n/s_{n})
, for
n
large the third term is upper bounded by a constant multiple of

s_{n}w_{-}^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{-})e^{M_{1}\sqrt{\log(n/s_{n})}}\leq nw_{-}^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{-}).

For the fourth term, by the same normal tail bound and the definition of
\zeta
we have
\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w_{-}))\asymp\phi(\zeta(w_{-}))/\zeta(w_{-})\asymp w_{-}g(\zeta(w_{-}))/\zeta(w_{-})
, which is of order
w_{-}(\zeta(w_{-}))^{-3}\asymp w_{-}/\zeta(w_{-})^{3}
, hence of order
w_{-}\tilde{m}(w_{-})/\zeta(w_{-})^{2}
because
\tilde{m}(w_{-})\asymp\zeta(w_{-})^{-1}
.
We deduce, recalling that
s_{n}=o(n)
implies automatically that
s_{n}\ll n(\log(n/s_{n}))^{-1/2}\asymp n\tilde{m}(w_{-})
,

V_{2}\lesssim nw_{-}^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{-})+w_{-}^{-1}s_{n}+nw_{-}^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{-})/\zeta(w_{-})^{2}\lesssim nw_{-}^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{-}),

so that

\frac{V_{2}+\mathcal{M}E/3}{E^{2}}\lesssim\frac{nw_{-}^{-1}\tilde{m}(w_{-})}{(\nu(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(w_{-}))^{2}}+\frac{1}{\nu w_{-}(n-s_{n})\tilde{m}(w_{-})}\lesssim\frac{1}{\nu^{2}nw_{-}\tilde{m}(w_{-})}.

This implies that

P_{\theta_{0}}(\hat{w}<w_{-})\leq e^{-c\nu^{2}nw_{-}\tilde{m}(w_{-})}

for some constant
c>0
.
Now, by Lemma
S-10
, we have
nw_{-}\tilde{m}(w_{-})\asymp s_{n}
, yielding the desired bound on the probability.
∎

We adapt Lemmas
S-10
and
S-11
slightly to apply even when
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})
is not bounded away from
1
or when
\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert<s_{n}
, in order to accommodate the settings of Theorems
S-11
and
7
. Recall the definition (
31
) of
\Theta^{\prime}_{b}
.

Lemma S-12
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
7
. For constants
C,D>0
let
\omega_{1}=C(\sigma_{n}/n)(\log(n/\sigma_{n}))^{1/2}
, where
\sigma_{n}=\max(s_{n},D\log n)
.
Then if
C
and
D
are suitably large, for all
n
large enough we have

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}}P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})\leq n^{-1}.

(S-45)

Proof.

We begin with a corresponding upper bound to (
S-44
). Using the bounds, found in Lemma
S-16
, that for some
c_{0},\omega_{0}>0
, any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and any
w<\omega_{0}

\tilde{m}(w)\geq c_{0}(\log(1/w))^{-1/2},\quad m_{1}(\mu,w)\leq 1/w,

we note that
\tilde{m}(\omega_{1})\asymp(\log(1/\omega_{1}))^{-1/2}\asymp(\log(n/\sigma_{n}))^{-1/2}
because
n/\sigma_{n}\to\infty
, and we further deduce that for
n
large enough, for any
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}_{b}
with support
S_{\theta}
of size
s_{\theta}\leq s_{n}
we have

\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\omega_{1})<(1-\nu)(n-s_{\theta})\tilde{m}(\omega_{1}),

(S-46)

provided the constant
C
in the definition of
\omega_{1}
is large enough.

We now argue as in proving Lemma
S-11
. Let
S=L^{\prime}
, for
L
as in (
S-28
), denote the score function. Necessarily
S(\hat{w})\geq 0
or
\hat{w}=0
, and we deduce that
\{\hat{w}>\omega_{1}\}\subset\{S(\omega_{1})>0\}
, hence

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})\leq P_{\theta}(S(\omega_{1})-E_{\theta}S(\omega_{1})>-E_{\theta}S(\omega_{1}))\\
=P_{\theta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}>-E\right),

where
W_{i}=\beta(X_{i},\omega_{1})-m_{1}(\theta_{i},\omega_{1})
and
E=E_{\theta}S(\omega_{1})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\omega_{1})
. For
n
large
|W_{i}|\leq\mathcal{M}:=2/\omega_{1}
a.s. (see Lemma
S-15
), so that we may apply Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
) and obtain

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})\leq e^{-0.5E^{2}/(V_{2}+\mathcal{M}E/3)},

where
V_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Var(W_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{2}(\theta_{i},\omega_{1})
, for
m_{2}(\theta_{i},w)=E_{\theta}(\beta(X_{i},w)^{2})
.
In view of (
S-46
),

-E=(n-s_{\theta})\tilde{m}(\omega_{1})-\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\omega_{1})>\nu(n-s_{\theta})\tilde{m}(\omega_{1}).

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
S-11
, one obtains

V_{2}\lesssim\omega_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}m_{1}(\theta_{i},\omega_{1})+\omega_{1}^{-1}s_{n}+n\omega_{1}^{-1}\tilde{m}(\omega_{1})+n\omega_{1}^{-1}\tilde{m}(\omega_{1})/\zeta(\omega_{1})^{2}\lesssim n\omega_{1}^{-1}\tilde{m}(\omega_{1}),

with the last bound following from (
S-46
) (note that
s_{n}\ll n
implies
s_{n}\ll n/\sqrt{\log(n/\sigma_{n})}
). Inserting the bounds for
E,

V_{2}
and
\mathcal{M}
into the obtained bound on
P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})
yields for a constant
c=c(\nu)

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})\leq e^{-cn\omega_{1}\tilde{m}(\omega_{1})}.

Recalling that
\tilde{m}(\omega_{1})\asymp(\log(n/\sigma_{n}))^{-1/2}

we see for
c^{\prime}
not depending on
D
that
P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})\leq e^{-c^{\prime}\sigma_{n}}
, and (
S-45
) follows upon choosing
D=D(c^{\prime})
large enough.
∎

S-8.3
A classification risk bound for the
\ell
-value procedure

In this section we prove that for any fixed real
b
and any
\eta>0

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})/s_{n}\geq\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta\right]=o(1),

and that the same holds with
\overline{\Phi}(b)
replaced by 0 when
b=b_{n}\to+\infty
or by 1 when
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
. This proves an upper bound of Theorem
S-11
to come (in Section
S-12
). Suppose
\theta\in\Theta_{b}
and let us write the classification loss
\lc(\theta,\varphi^{\hat{\ell}})
as the sum
V+(s_{n}-S)
, where as in the proof of Theorem
S-6
we write
V=V(\hat{w})=\sum_{i\not\in S_{\theta}}\varphi^{\hat{\ell}}_{i}
,
S=S(\hat{w})=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}{\varphi^{\hat{\ell}}_{i}}
, with
S_{\theta}=\{i:\theta_{i}\neq 0\}
denoting the support of
\theta
. It suffices to show that uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta_{b}
, for some positive sequences
\xi_{n}\to 0
and
\nu_{n}\to 0
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\left[s_{n}-S\geq(\overline{\Phi}(b)+\xi_{n})s_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=o(1),

(S-47)

\displaystyle P_{\theta}\big[V>\nu_{n}s_{n}\big]

\displaystyle=o(1),

(S-48)

To prove (
S-47
), note that we may apply Theorem
S-6
and arguments in the proof thereof with
\bm{a}=(a_{n}^{*}+b,\dots,a_{n}^{*}+b)
. In the case of a fixed
b\in\mathbb{R}
, recall that we argued in proving Theorem
S-6
(see before (
S-39
)) that
on the event
\hat{w}\geq w_{-}
, we have

s_{n}-S\leq N
, for
N
a Poisson binomial
N
with parameter vector
\bm{p}=(\overline{\Phi}(a_{j}-a_{n}^{*})+\xi_{n})_{j\leq s_{n}}
and for a sequence
\xi_{n}\to 0
; for the current choice of
\bm{a}
we see that
N\sim\text{Bin}(s_{n},\overline{\Phi}(b)+\xi_{n})
.
Then (
S-47
) follows by recalling that
P(\hat{w}<w_{-})\to 0
from Lemma
S-11
, and by applying Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
). In the case
b=b_{n}\to+\infty
the same argument applies upon replacing
\overline{\Phi}(b)
by
0=\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(1)
. In the case
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
, since
S\geq 0
we automatically have

P_{\theta}\big[s_{n}-S\geq(1+\delta_{n})s_{n}\big]=0,

so that (
S-47
) holds in all cases (with suitable substitutions).

To prove (
S-48
), one could similarly use the bound (
S-42
). Here, to allow for the case
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
, we adapt this bound by appealing to Lemma
S-12
, which yields the existence of constants
C,D
such that for large
n

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})\leq n^{-1},\quad\omega_{1}=C\frac{\sigma_{n}}{n}\big(\log(n/\sigma_{n})\big)^{1/2},\penalty\ \sigma_{n}=\max(s_{n},D\log n).

Then, writing

V^{\prime}=\#\{i\not\in S_{\theta}:\ell_{i,\omega_{1}}<t\},

we apply Lemmas
S-14
and
S-19
as in proving Theorem
S-6
to deduce that

E_{\theta}V^{\prime}\lesssim n\omega_{1}\log(1/\omega_{1})^{-3/2}\lesssim\sigma_{n}/\log(n/\sigma_{n}),

where we have used that
n/\sigma_{n}\to\infty
to see that
\log(1/\omega_{1})\asymp\log(n/\sigma_{n})
. When
\sigma_{n}=D\log(n)
we note that
\sigma_{n}/\log(n/\sigma_{n})
is upper bounded by a constant.
For some
C^{\prime}>0
we therefore have

E_{\theta}V\leq nP_{\theta}(\hat{w}>\omega_{1})+E_{\theta}V^{\prime}\leq 1+E_{\theta}V^{\prime}\leq C^{\prime}\max\Big(1,\frac{s_{n}}{\log(n/s_{n})}\Big)=o(s_{n}),

(S-49)

where we have used that
s_{n}\to\infty
and
n/s_{n}\to\infty
.
Applying Markov’s inequality we deduce
P_{\theta}[V>\nu_{n}s_{n}]\leq\nu_{n}^{-1}s_{n}^{-1}E_{\theta}V
tends to zero if
\nu_{n}
tends to zero sufficiently slowly.
This proves (
S-48
), including the cases
b=b_{n}\to\pm\infty
, and thus concludes the proof.

Remark S-11
.

A straightforward corollary of the above proof shows that the
\ell
-value procedure is sparsity preserving. It suffices to show that
P(V+S>2s_{n})=o(1)
, or that
P(V>s_{n})=o(1)
, which follows from the proof, taking
\nu_{n}=1
.

Remark S-12
.

Let us provide an alternative proof of the
\ell
-value upper bound part of Theorem
7
, similar in spirit to the just obtained in-probability bounds. Write
S_{\theta}=\{i:\theta_{i}\neq 0\}
,
s_{\theta}=\lvert S_{\theta}\rvert
,

V=V(\hat{w})=\sum_{i\not\in S_{\theta}}\varphi^{\hat{\ell}}_{i}
and
S=S(\hat{w})=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\varphi^{\hat{\ell}}_{i}
, and note as in the previous proof of Theorem
7
(in Section
S-6.3
) that it suffices to show

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})}E_{\theta}V=o(s_{n}),\quad\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})}E_{\theta}[s_{\theta}-S]\leq s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(s_{n}).

The first of these follows from taking a supremum in (
S-49
), whose proof we note applies for
\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})
not just
\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s_{n})
.

The second is trivial in the case
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
since
0\leq s_{\theta}-S\leq s_{\theta}\leq s_{n}
for
\theta\in\Theta_{b}^{\prime}(s_{n})
. In the cases
b\in\mathbb{R}
fixed and
b=b_{n}\to+\infty
, note firstly an examination of the proofs of Lemmas
S-10
and
S-11
reveals that for some constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
, for any
\theta\in\Theta_{b}(s)
with
s\leq s_{n}
we have

P_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})\leq e^{-cs},\quad w_{-}:=c^{\prime}(s/n)(\log(n/s))^{1/2}.

In this setting the bound (
S-39
) reads that, for some sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
which can be chosen independently of
s
,

\displaystyle E_{\theta}[s-S]

\displaystyle\leq sP_{\theta}(\hat{w}<w_{-})+s(\overline{\Phi}(b)+\delta_{n})

\displaystyle\leq se^{-cs}+s(\overline{\Phi}(b)+\delta_{n})\leq C(c)+s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(b)+o(s_{n}).

The bound at stake then follows by taking the maximum over
0\leq s\leq s_{n}
in the last display.

S-8.4
Background material for the
\ell
-value procedure

We gather results from
[
19
]
which are used in the proofs for this section. Some of these results were originally formulated with dependence on
g
and a related parameter
\kappa\in[1,2]
; as in
[
2
]
, we simplify such expressions here by substituting the explicit form (
S-26
) for
g
, which has
\kappa=2
, and using the bounds
\sup_{x}\lvert g(x)\rvert\leq 1/\sqrt{2\pi}
and, for
\lvert x\rvert\geq 2
,
x^{-2}/(2\sqrt{2\pi})\leq g(x)\leq x^{-2}/\sqrt{2\pi}
.

Lemma S-13
.

The following functions are strictly decreasing (with probability 1 in the case of random functions).

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto S(w)=L^{\prime}(w),

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto\ell_{i,w}(X),

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto-\tilde{m}(w),

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto m_{1}(\tau,w),\quad\tau\in\mathbb{R}\text{ fixed,}

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto\xi(u)=(\phi/g)^{-1}(u),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto\zeta(u).

These monotonicity results can be found in
[
19
]
, and see
[
2
, Lemma 4]
for most proofs collected in one place.

Lemma S-14

(Proposition 3 in
[
19
]
)
.

P_{\theta}(\ell_{i,w}\leq t)\leq 2r(w,t)\xi(r(w,t))^{-3}
, where
r(w,t)=wt(1-w)^{-1}(1-t)^{-1}
and
\xi
is as in (
S-35
).

Lemma S-15

(Lemma S-20 in
[
19
]
)
.

Define
\beta(x,w)
as in (
S-31
). Then there exists
c_{1}>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
w\in(0,1]
,
\lvert\beta(x,w)\rvert\leq(\min(w,c_{1}))^{-1}
.

Lemma S-16

(Lemmas S-21, S-23 and S-27 in
[
19
]
, and using Lemma
S-18
below)
.

Define
\tilde{m}
and
m_{1}
as in (
S-32
), (
S-33
). Then
\tilde{m}
is continuous, non-negative and increasing. For fixed
\tau
the function
w\mapsto m_{1}(\tau,w)
is continuous and decreasing.
There exist
\omega_{0},c,c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and all
w<\omega_{0}

\displaystyle c(\log(1/w))^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{m}(w)

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}(\log(1/w))^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\phantom{\leq}m_{1}(\tau,w)

\displaystyle\leq 1/w.

There exist constants
M_{0},C_{1}>0
and
\omega_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for any
w\leq\omega_{0}
, and any
\mu\geq M_{0}
, with
T_{\mu}(w)=1+\lvert\mu\rvert^{-1}\lvert\zeta(w)-\lvert\mu\rvert\rvert
,

\displaystyle m_{1}(\mu,w)

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\frac{\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w)-\mu)}{w}T_{\mu}(w).

Lemma S-17

(Lemma S-26 and Corollary S-28 in
[
19
]
)
.

Define
m_{2}
as in (
S-34
).
There exist constants
C>0
and
\omega_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for
any
w\leq\omega_{0}
and any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,

m_{2}(\mu,w)\leq C\frac{\overline{\Phi}(\zeta(w)-\lvert\mu\rvert)}{w^{2}}.

There exist
M_{0},C^{\prime}>0
and
\omega_{0}\in(0,1)

such that for any
w\leq\omega_{0}
and any
\mu\geq M_{0}

m_{2}(\mu,w)\leq C^{\prime}\frac{m_{1}(\mu,w)}{w}.

Lemma S-18

(Lemma S-14 in
[
19
]
)
.

Consider
\zeta(w)
as in (
S-36
).
Then
\zeta(w)\sim(2\log(1/w))^{1/2}
as
w\to 0
. More precisely, for constants
c,C\in\mathbb{R}
and for
w
small enough,

(2\log(1/w)+2\log\log(1/w)+c)^{1/2}\leq\zeta(w)\leq(2\log(1/w)+2\log\log(1/w)+C)^{1/2}.

Lemma S-19

(Lemma S-12 in
[
19
]
)
.

Consider
\xi
as in (
S-35
).
Then
\xi(u)\sim(2\log(1/u))^{1/2}
, and more precisely, for
u
small enough,

\displaystyle\xi(u)

\displaystyle\geq\Big(2\log(1/u)+2\log\log(1/u)+2\log 2\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\xi(u)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(2\log(1/u)+2\log\log(1/u)+6\log 2\Big)^{1/2}.

The above bounds come from Lemma S-12 in
[
19
]

\displaystyle\xi(u)

\displaystyle\geq\left(-2\log u-2\log g\left(\sqrt{-2\log(Cu)}\right)-\log(2\pi)\right)^{1/2};

\displaystyle\xi(u)

\displaystyle\leq\left(-2\log u-2\log g\left(\sqrt{-4\log u}\right)-\log(2\pi)\right)^{1/2},

where
C=\sqrt{2\pi}\sup_{x}\lvert g(x)\rvert
,
by using
x^{-2}/(2\sqrt{2\pi})\leq g(x)\leq x^{-2}/(\sqrt{2\pi})
for
\lvert x\rvert\geq 2
.
For instance, for the lower bound we use

\displaystyle-2\log g\left(\sqrt{-2\log(Cu)}\right)-\log(2\pi)

\displaystyle\geq 2\log(\sqrt{2\pi})+2\log(-2\log(Cu))-\log(2\pi)

\displaystyle\geq 2\log(2)+2\log(\log(1/u)+\log(1/C))

\displaystyle\geq 2\log 2+2\log(\log(1/u)),

where the last inequality holds because
C\leq 1
.

S-9
Large signals: adaptation and proofs

In this section, we first discuss adaptation in the large signal regime and provide the precise statements corresponding to Theorem
9
. Then we turn to the proofs of both non-adaptive and adaptive results.

Recall some notation for the Subbotin location model (Example
2
). For
\zeta>1
, the parameter set
\Theta(r,\beta)
is defined by (
32
)–(
33
), with
M=M(r)=(\zeta r\log{n})^{1/\zeta}
and sparsity parameter
a\leq s_{n}/n^{1-\beta}\leq b
(which we may sometimes informally write
s_{n}\asymp n^{1-\beta}
), for
r>\beta
and
a,b>0
. Define

v_{R}=n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}=v_{C}/n^{1-\beta},

(S-50)

where
\kappa=\kappa(r,\beta,\zeta)
is the solution of the equation

(r^{1/\zeta}-\kappa^{1/\zeta})^{\zeta}-\kappa=\beta.

(S-51)

Recall from Theorem
8
that
\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\asymp v_{R}
so that
v_{R}=v_{R}(r,\beta,\zeta)
is the target rate for the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk; similarly

v_{C}=v_{C}(r,\beta,\zeta)=n^{1-\beta}v_{R}
is the target rate for the classification loss.

S-9.1
Impossibility of full simultaneous adaptation to
s_{n}
and
r

The following is a negative result showing that it is not possible to fully adapt to both signal strength
r
and sparsity parameter
s_{n}
(or
\beta
).

Theorem S-7

(Impossibility of
(r,\beta)
–adaptation)
.

Consider the Subbotin location model (Example
2
) for some
\zeta>1
and classes
\Theta(r,\beta)
as in (
32
)–(
33
) for
\beta\in(0,1)
and
r>\beta
. Suppose
r_{1},r_{2}>0
verify

r_{1}>r_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa(r_{2},\beta,\zeta)>1-\beta,

for
\kappa
as in (
S-51
).
Then there exists
c>0
(depending on
r_{1},r_{2},\beta,\zeta
) such that, for
v_{C},v_{R}
as in (
S-50
),

\,\inf_{\varphi}\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r_{i},\beta)}\,\frac{\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)}{v_{R}(r_{i},\beta,\zeta)}\geq c\{v_{C}(\beta,r_{1},\zeta)^{-1}n^{-r_{2}}\}\wedge v_{C}(\beta,r_{2},\zeta)^{-1}.

On the two–class adaptation problem
\theta\in\Theta(r_{1},\beta)\cup\Theta(r_{2},\beta)
, the best possible rate incurs a polynomial loss compared to the non-adaptive rate whenever
\kappa(\beta,r_{1},\zeta)-r_{2}>1-\beta
. The same result holds for the classification risk, with
\mathfrak{R}/v_{R}
in the last display replaced by
E_{\theta}\lc/v_{C}
.

The proof of Theorem
S-7
can be found in Section
S-9.6
.

To fix ideas, let us discuss the case of Gaussian noise
\zeta=2
. The regime of
r
’s for which
\kappa(r,\beta,2)\geq 1-\beta
can then explicitly be written as the range of
r
’s with
\sqrt{r}>1+\sqrt{1-\beta}
, as follows from examining the expression for
\kappa
in the Gaussian case. This corresponds to the regime of ‘exact recovery’ (for which the classification risk goes to
0
). In this regime, the target rate (either
E_{\theta}\lc
or
\mathfrak{R}
) is very fast, so even only one missclassification can have a large impact on the risk. And indeed, the idea behind the lower bound is to build two vectors identical except on one coordinate: when the difference between signal strengths on this coordinate is large enough, then the smallest one can be mistaken for noise with a probability that becomes comparable to or higher than the target rate (which goes to
0
in that regime), hence the impossibility.

This provides a simple example where there is a polynomial loss due to adaptation. This striking result appears to be in part due to the discreteness of the considered risks: note for instance that considering the problem in probability instead of in expectation, the impossibility disappears, as in probability
P_{\theta}(\lc\geq\delta_{n})=P_{\theta}(\lc\geq 1-\delta)
for any small
\delta
and
\delta_{n}=o(1)
. For versions in probability for the large signal regime we refer to Section
S-12.2
.

If one restricts the range of possible signal strengths to
\kappa(r,\beta,\zeta)<1-\beta
, then adaptation to both
\beta
and
r
becomes possible, see Section
S-9.4
.

S-9.2
Adaptation: known sparsity, unknown
r
and top–
K
procedures

In this section, we assume that the number of
nonzero
coefficients of
\theta
is
known
. Recall

\Theta_{=}(r,s_{n})=\{\theta\in\ell_{0}[s_{n}]:\ |S_{\theta}|=s_{n},\ \ |\theta_{i}|\geq M(r)\text{ for all }i\in S_{\theta}\},

(S-52)

which differs from
\Theta(r,\beta)
by the fact that support of
\theta
has cardinality
exactly

s_{n}
(instead of of order
n^{1-\beta}
). It is reasonable (in fact necessary) to assume this, since it follows from the proof of Theorem
S-7
that when
r
is unknown, full adaptation to signal strength is impossible as soon as the class contains vectors of supports differing in cardinality by just
1
.

For
K\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the top–
K
procedure is defined as
\varphi_{i}{[K]}=\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|\geq|X|_{(K)}\}
: it rejects the coordinates corresponding to the
K
largest signals (since the noise distribution has a density, there are almost surely no ties among observations and so it is uniquely defined with probability
1
).

Theorem S-8
.

For any
1\leq s_{n}\leq n
, the top–
s_{n}
procedure
\varphi_{i}{[s_{n}]}=\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|\geq|X|_{(s_{n})}\}
verifies, for any
\theta\in\ell_{0}[s_{n}]\setminus\ell_{0}[s_{n}-1]
,

E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi[s_{n}])\leq 2\inf_{T\in\mathcal{T}}E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,T),

where
\mathcal{T}
is the set of all thresholding procedures with deterministic threshold.

Theorem
S-8
states that the top–
s_{n}
procedure is within a factor
2
of the rate of the best (deterministic–) threshold-based procedure. Since Theorem
8
shows that the procedure that thresholds deterministically at
t_{n}^{*}
given by (
34
) achieves the rate
v_{R}
over
\Theta(r,\beta)
, the following corollary immediately follows.

Corollary S-2
.

Consider the Subbotin location model (Example
2
) for some
\zeta>1
and let
\Theta_{=}(r,s_{n})
be as in (
S-52
), for
M=M(r)=(\zeta r\log{n})^{1/\zeta}
and
s_{n}\asymp n^{1-\beta}
for
r>\beta
.
There exists a constant
c=c(r,\beta,\zeta)
such that, for large enough
n
and
v_{C},v_{R}
as in (
S-50
),

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{=}(r,s_{n})}E_{\theta}\lc(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\leq cv_{C}(r,\beta,\zeta),

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{=}(r,s_{n})}E_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\leq cv_{R}(r,\beta,\zeta).

Remark S-13
.

Theorem
8
is stated for the class
\Theta(r,\beta)
. Although in principle the lower bound could be faster for the smaller class
\Theta_{=}(r,s_{n})
, we expect that both rates coincide. In any case, the optimal rate for
thresholding–based procedures
(with possibly random threshold) over
\Theta_{=}(r,s_{n})
is
v_{R}
(this follow by similar arguments as in
[
42
]
, Corollary 1, where the authors consider a slightly different, quasi–Subbotin noise). This shows that adaptation is achievable at least for arbitrary thresholding procedures, and otherwise shows that the rate (at least)
v_{R}
can be achieved adaptively.

We now present a result on top-
K
procedures which is concerned with lower signal strength and is a corollary of both Theorem
3
on no-tradeoff and Theorem
S-8
. It shows that the oracle top–
s_{n}
procedure, despite being rate-optimal for the large signal regime, is sub-optimal for weaker signals. This suboptimality comes from the fact that its risk splits equally among false positive and false negatives, which implies the result once one notes the procedure is sparsity-preserving, hence illustrating the versatility of Theorem
3
(and more generally Theorem
6
).

Corollary S-3

(Exact risk of the top
s_{n}
–procedure in the boundary case)
.

In the setting of Theorem
3
,
consider the class of signals
\Theta_{b}=\Theta(a_{b},s_{n})
for some fixed real number
b
. For
\varphi
the top
s_{n}
–procedure, as
n\to\infty
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}E_{\theta}\lc(\varphi,\theta)/s_{n}\sim\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\mathfrak{R}(\varphi,\theta)\sim 2\bar{\Phi}(b).

In particular, the top
s_{n}
–procedure is rate-optimal but not sharp minimax optimal over
\Theta_{b}
. A similar result holds in Subbotin noise of index
\zeta>1
, with
\bar{\Phi}(b)
replaced by
\bar{\Phi}_{\zeta}(b)
.

S-9.3
Adaptation in Gaussian noise: unknown sparsity, known
r

Let us consider the class
\Theta(r,\beta)
as in (
32
), assuming that the minimal signal strength
r>\beta
is
known
. Then adaptation is possible: the idea is to use a plug-in procedure where
\beta
is estimated first.
In this and the next Section
S-9.4
we focus on the case of Gaussian noise for simplicity. Note that in Gaussian noise the equation (
S-51
) defining
\kappa
is solved as

\sqrt{\kappa(r,\beta)}=(\sqrt{r}-\beta/\sqrt{r})/2.

In Gaussian noise, the optimal threshold
t_{n}^{*}
in (
34
) writes, for
\beta\in(0,1)
and
r>\beta
,

\tau(r,\beta)=\left(\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{\kappa(r,\beta)}\right)\sqrt{2\log{n}}.

(S-53)

If we have an estimator
\hat{\beta}
of
\beta
, for
r
known
\tau(r,\hat{\beta})=(\sqrt{r}/2+\hat{\beta}/(2\sqrt{r}))\sqrt{2\log{n}}
is an estimator of the optimal threshold.

Theorem S-9

(Known
r
)
.

Consider the Gaussian location model (Example
1
) and the class
\Theta(r,\beta)
as in (
32
)–(
33
) for
\beta\in(0,1)
. Let
r>0
be given. There exists an estimator
\hat{\beta}
such that, if one sets

\varphi_{i}^{r}=1_{\{|X_{i}|\geq(\sqrt{r}/2+\hat{\beta}/(2\sqrt{r}))\sqrt{2\log{n}}\}}.

then for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)>0
such that for
n
large enough, for any
\beta\in[\varepsilon,\min(r,1)-\varepsilon]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}E_{\theta}\lc(\varphi^{r},\theta)

\displaystyle\leq Cv_{C}(r,\beta),

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}E_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}(\varphi^{r},\theta)

\displaystyle\leq Cv_{R}(r,\beta).

The role of
\varepsilon>0
in the statement of Theorem
S-9
is to ensure a minimal separation from the different regime of weaker signals as in Sections
2
–
3
.
The proof of Theorem
S-9
is given in Section
S-9.9
.

S-9.4
Adaptation: simultaneous adaptation in regime
\kappa<1-\beta

The next result shows that adaptation is possible in a range of pairs
(\beta,r)
that is arbitrarily close to those satisfying the constraints
\kappa(r,\beta,2)<1-\beta
– in Gaussian noise the latter writes
\sqrt{r}<1+\sqrt{1-\beta}
– and
\kappa>\beta
, in order to be in the ‘large signal’ regime. Similarly as for adaptation for known
r
, we introduce the set, for
\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small,

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}(\varepsilon)=\left\{(\beta,r):\ \,\beta\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon],\ \,\beta+\varepsilon\leq r\leq(1+\sqrt{1-\beta})^{2}-\varepsilon)\right\}.

(S-54)

Below we will use that for any admissibly
r
in the above set,
r<4
.

Theorem S-10

(Adaptation over the range

\sqrt{\beta}<\sqrt{r}<1+\sqrt{1-\beta}
)
.

Consider the Gaussian location model (Example
1
) and the class
\Theta(r,\beta)
as in (
32
)–(
33
). Let
\varepsilon>0
.
There exists a procedure
\varphi=\varphi(X)
such that, for
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}(\varepsilon)
as in (
S-54
), there exists a finite
C=C(a,b,\varepsilon)>0
such that, for
n
large enough,

\sup_{(\beta,r)\in\mathcal{J}}\ \sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}\frac{E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)}{v_{C}(r,\beta)}\leq C,

and the same holds for the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk, upon replacing
E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi)/v_{C}
by
\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)/v_{R}
.

The proof of Theorem
S-10
is given in Section
S-9.10
.

S-9.5
Proof of minimax rate for large signals, Theorem
8

First, we let, for
r>\beta>0
,

\Psi(\cdot;r,\beta):u\in(0,r)\mapsto\Psi(u;r,\beta)=(r^{1/\zeta}-u^{1/\zeta})^{\zeta}-u-\beta.

(S-55)

Clearly,
\Psi
is continuous decreasing, with
\Psi(0;r,\beta)=r-\beta>0
and
\Psi(r/2^{\zeta};r,\beta)=-\beta<0
. Hence,
\kappa\in(0,r/2^{\zeta})
such that
\Psi(\kappa;r,\beta)=0
is well defined.

Upper bound

First, if
V^{*}=\sum_{i\notin S_{\theta}}\varphi^{*}
is the number of false discoveries of
\varphi^{*}
, we have
V^{*}\sim\text{Bin}(n-|S_{\theta}|,2\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(t_{n}^{*}))
.
Hence, for
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
,

\displaystyle E_{\theta}V^{*}

\displaystyle=2(n-|S_{\theta}|)\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(t_{n}^{*})\asymp ne^{-(t_{n}^{*})^{\zeta}/\zeta}/(t_{n}^{*})^{\zeta-1}\asymp n^{1-\beta-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta},

because
(t_{n}^{*})^{\zeta}=\zeta(\beta+\kappa)\log n
and
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)\asymp e^{-x^{\zeta}/\zeta}/x^{\zeta-1}
as
x\to\infty
by Lemma
S-28
.

Second, if
S^{*}=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\varphi^{*}_{i}
is the number of true discoveries of
\varphi^{*}
, for
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
,

\displaystyle E_{\theta}S^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}P(|\varepsilon_{i}+\theta_{i}|\geq t_{n}^{*})=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\big\{P(\varepsilon_{i}\geq t_{n}^{*}-\theta_{i})+P(-\varepsilon_{i}\geq t_{n}^{*}+\theta_{i})\big\}

\displaystyle\geq|S_{\theta}|\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(t_{n}^{*}-M(r)).

Hence, for any
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
,

\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\leq 1-\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(t_{n}^{*}-M(r))=\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(M(r)-t_{n}^{*})=\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}((\zeta\kappa\log n)^{1/\zeta}).

Hence,

\FNR(\theta,\varphi)\lesssim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta},
because by Lemma
S-28
we have
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)\asymp e^{-x^{\zeta}/\zeta}/x^{\zeta-1}
when
x\to\infty
.

For the FDR term, we need additional concentration arguments.
Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
) gives that for any
t>0
, we have

P_{\theta}[|S^{*}-E_{\theta}S^{*}|\geq t]\leq 2\exp\left\{-t^{2}/(2|S_{\theta}|(1+o(1))+2t/3)\right\}\leq 2\exp(-cs_{n}^{1/2}),

by taking
t=|S_{\theta}|^{3/4}
and a constant
c>0
small enough.
This gives
S^{*}\geq s_{n}(1+o(1))
with probability at least
1-2\exp(-cs_{n}^{1/2})
.
We now distinguish between the two following cases.

Case:
1-(\beta+\kappa)>0

In that case, Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
) provides that
V^{*}
satisfies the following concentration property:

P_{\theta}[|V^{*}-E_{\theta}V^{*}|\geq M\sqrt{E_{\theta}V^{*}}]\leq 2\exp\left\{-c[M^{2}\wedge(M\sqrt{E_{\theta}V^{*}})]\right\}.

Taking
M
such that
M\sqrt{E_{\theta}V^{*}}=n^{(1-\beta-\kappa)3/4}
(i.e.
M\asymp n^{(1-\beta-\kappa)/4}(\log n)^{(1-1/\zeta)/2}
which tends to
\infty
since
\kappa<1-\beta
) gives
V^{*}\leq E_{\theta}V^{*}+n^{(1-\beta-\kappa)3/4}
with probability at least
1-2e^{-cn^{(1-\beta-\kappa)/4}}
.
Hence, by the above concentration arguments, we have for all
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
,

\displaystyle\FDR(\theta,\varphi^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{E_{\theta}[V^{*}]+n^{(1-\beta-\kappa)3/4}}{s_{n}(1+o(1))-s_{n}^{3/4}}+2e^{-cn^{(1-\beta-\kappa)/4}}+2e^{-cs_{n}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+s_{n}^{-1/4})(n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}+n^{-(3/4)\kappa})+2e^{-cn^{(1-\beta-\kappa)/4}}+2e^{-cs_{n}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}.

Case:
1-(\beta+\kappa)\leq 0

In this case, we still use the concentration of
S^{*}
but circumvent the concentration of
V^{*}
by upper bounding the FDR with the following more direct argument: for all
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
,

\displaystyle\FDR(\theta,\varphi^{*})

\displaystyle\leq E_{\theta}[V^{*}/((1+o(1))s_{n}]+2e^{-cs_{n}^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}.

This entails the result in both cases.

Lower bound

We propose to establish the lower bound

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\gtrsim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}.

(S-56)

For this, we apply Theorem
S-3

with
\rho=1
and the prior
\pi=((1-w)\delta_{0}+w\delta_{M})^{\otimes n}
,
M=M(r)=(\zeta r\log n)^{1/\zeta}
,
w=\frac{a+b}{2}/n^{\beta}
.

Case:
1-(\beta+\kappa)>0

We apply the bound (i) in Theorem
S-3
for
\rho=1
,
s_{n}=bn^{1-\beta}
and some
\eta=\eta_{n}\to 0
to be chosen later.
This gives, with
M_{\rho}=M_{1}
here,

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\frac{M_{\rho}}{2bn^{1-\beta}+M_{\rho}}(1-e^{-c\eta_{n}^{2}M_{\rho}})-nP_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>bn^{1-\beta})-2P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta(r,\beta)),

for some universal constant
c>0
, where

M_{\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>1/2],
for
\ell_{i}(X)=P_{\pi}(\theta_{i}=0\>|\>X)
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
By definition of
\Theta(r,\beta)
and of the prior
\pi
, we have
P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta(r,\beta))\leq P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>bn^{1-\beta})+P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}<an^{1-\beta})
.
Also, we have

\displaystyle(n+2)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>bn^{1-\beta})

\displaystyle\leq(n+2)P(\mathcal{B}(n,w)-nw>((b-a)/2)n^{1-\beta})

\displaystyle\leq(n+2)e^{-c^{\prime}n^{1-\beta}}\leq 1/n^{\kappa+1},

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
and for
n
large enough. Similarly, we have
(n+2)P_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}<an^{1-\beta})\leq 1/n^{\kappa+1}
.

Next, we have by a direct computation

\ell_{i}(X)=P_{\pi}(\theta_{i}=0\>|\>X)=\frac{(1-w)\phi_{\zeta}(X_{i})}{(1-w)\phi_{\zeta}(X_{i})+w\phi_{\zeta}(X_{i}-M)},

so that
\ell_{i}(X)>1/2
if and only if
\phi_{\zeta}(X_{i}-M)/\phi_{\zeta}(X_{i})<(1-w)/w
, that is if and only if

|X_{i}|^{\zeta}-|X_{i}-M|^{\zeta}<\zeta\log(1/w-1)
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle P_{\pi}[\ell_{i}(X)>1/2\>|\>\theta_{i}\neq 0]

\displaystyle=P[|M+\varepsilon|^{\zeta}-|\varepsilon|^{\zeta}<\zeta\log(1/w-1)]

\displaystyle\geq P[|M+\varepsilon|^{\zeta}-|\varepsilon|^{\zeta}<\zeta\log(n^{\beta}/b-1)],

where
\varepsilon\sim\phi_{\zeta}
and by using that
w\leq b/n^{\beta}
.
Recalling
\Psi(\cdot;r,\beta)
defined by (
S-55
), we obtain

\displaystyle P_{\pi}[\ell_{i}(X)>1/2\>|\>\theta_{i}\neq 0]

\displaystyle\geq P[|M+\varepsilon|^{\zeta}-|\varepsilon|^{\zeta}<\zeta\log(n^{\beta}/b-1)]

\displaystyle\geq P[\varepsilon\in(-M,0),(M+\varepsilon)^{\zeta}-(-\varepsilon)^{\zeta}<\zeta\log(n^{\beta}/b-1)]

\displaystyle=P[\varepsilon\in(0,M),(M-\varepsilon)^{\zeta}-\varepsilon^{\zeta}-\zeta\beta\log n<\zeta\log(n^{\beta}/b-1)-\zeta\beta\log n]

\displaystyle=P\left(\varepsilon\in(0,M),\Psi\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{\zeta}}{\zeta\log n};r,\beta\right)<\Psi(\kappa_{n})\right),

where
\kappa_{n}=\Psi^{-1}\left(\frac{\log(n^{\beta}/b-1)-\beta\log n}{\log n}\right)=\kappa+O(1/\log n)
.
By the properties of
\Psi
, we get for
n
large enough that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}[\ell_{i}(X)>1/2\>|\>\theta_{i}\neq 0]

\displaystyle\geq P\left(M>\varepsilon>(\zeta\kappa_{n}\log n)^{1/\zeta}\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}((\kappa_{n}\zeta\log n)^{1/\zeta})-\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}((r\zeta\log n)^{1/\zeta}).

Now using Lemma
S-28
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}((r\zeta\log n)^{1/\zeta})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi_{\zeta}((r\zeta\log n)^{1/\zeta})}{(r\zeta\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}}\lesssim n^{-r}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}((\kappa_{n}\zeta\log n)^{1/\zeta})

\displaystyle\geq\zeta^{-1}\frac{\phi_{\zeta}(x)}{x^{\zeta-1}}=\zeta^{-1}n^{-\kappa_{n}}/(\kappa_{n}\zeta\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}\gtrsim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}.

Since
\kappa<r
, it follows that

P_{\pi}(\ell_{i}(X)>1/2,\theta_{i}\neq 0)=wP_{\pi}(\ell_{i}(X)>1/2\mid\theta_{i}\neq 0)\gtrsim n^{-\beta}n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta},

(S-57)

and hence

\displaystyle M_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{\pi}[\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>1/2]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}wP_{\pi}[\ell_{i}(X)>1/2\>|\>\theta_{i}\neq 0]

\displaystyle\gtrsim n^{1-\beta}n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}.

(S-58)

This also gives, because
1-(\beta+\kappa)>0
by assumption,

e^{-c\eta_{n}^{2}M_{\rho}}\leq e^{-c^{\prime}\eta_{n}^{2}n^{1-(\beta+\kappa)}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}}=o(1),

by choosing
\eta_{n}=1/\log n
. Combining the relations obtained above gives

\frac{M_{\rho}}{2bn^{1-\beta}+M_{\rho}}(1-e^{-c\eta_{n}^{2}M_{\rho}})\gtrsim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}

and shows (
S-56
).

Case:
1-(\beta+\kappa)\leq 0

We apply the bound (ii) in Theorem
S-3
with
\rho=1
and
s_{n}=bn^{1-\beta}
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{bn^{1-\beta}+1}\wedge\frac{1}{bn^{1-\beta}}\right)m_{\pi}-nP_{\pi}(\lVert\theta\rVert_{0}>bn^{1-\beta})-2P_{\pi}(\theta\notin\Theta(r,\beta))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m_{\pi}}{1+bn^{1-\beta}}-1/n^{\kappa+1},

for
m_{\pi}=P_{\pi}(\exists i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\>:\>\theta_{i}\neq 0,\ell_{i}(X)>1/2)
, as in the previous case. By using the computation (
S-57
) from the previous case, we have

\displaystyle m_{\pi}

\displaystyle=P_{\pi}(\exists i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\>:\>\theta_{i}\neq 0\,,\,\ell_{i}(X)>1/2)

\displaystyle=1-(1-P_{\pi}(\theta_{1}\neq 0,\ell_{1}(X)>1/2))^{n}

\displaystyle\geq 1-(1-Cn^{-\beta}n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta})^{n}

for some constant
C>0
. Since
1-(\beta+\kappa)\leq 0
, we have
Cn^{-\beta}n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}=o(1)
, hence

1-(1-Cn^{-\beta}n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta})^{n}\sim Cn^{1-\beta-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta},

and the result follows.

To completely finish the proof, it remains to prove the same bounds for the classification risk
\lc/n^{1-\beta}
. The upper bound is completely analogous since

\lc(\theta,\varphi^{*})/n^{1-\beta}=E_{\theta}V^{*}/n^{1-\beta}+(|S_{\theta}|-E_{\theta}S^{*})/n^{1-\beta}\lesssim n^{-\kappa}/(\log n)^{1-1/\zeta}.

As for the lower bound, we apply Theorem
S-5

with the same prior, which gives the correct bound by using (
S-58
) (which holds beyond the case
1-(\beta+\kappa)>0
).

S-9.6
Proof of Theorem
S-7
[Thm
9
, (i)]

Set
\Theta_{i}=\Theta(r_{i},\beta)
for
i=1,2
. We start with the result for
\lc
. Set
v_{i}=v_{C}(r_{i},\beta,\zeta),i=1,2
as a shorthand.
It is enough to bound from below, for
\theta_{1}\in\Theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Theta_{2}
, and any procedure
\varphi
, the quantity

\Sigma:=\Sigma(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\varphi):=\frac{E_{\theta_{1}}\lc(\theta_{1},\varphi)}{v_{1}}+\frac{E_{\theta_{2}}\lc(\theta_{2},\varphi)}{v_{2}}.

Let us
s_{n,1},s_{n,2}
be two consecutive integers in
[an^{1-\beta},bn^{1-\beta}]
, so that
s_{n,1}=s_{n,2}-1
(they exist for large
n
since
a<b
). Define

\theta_{1,i}=\begin{cases}\,M(r_{1})&\quad\text{if }\ 1\leq i\leq s_{n,1},\\
\,0&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Define
\theta_{2}
to be the same as
\theta_{1}
, except for
i=s_{n,1}+1=s_{n,2}
, for which one sets
\theta_{2,s_{n,2}}=M(r_{2})
. Using a change of measure, one can write

\displaystyle E_{\theta_{1}}\lc(\theta_{1},\varphi)

\displaystyle=E_{\theta_{2}}\left[\lc(\theta_{1},\varphi(X))\phi_{\zeta}(X_{s_{n,2}})/\phi_{\zeta}(X_{s_{n,2}}-M(r_{2}))\right]

\displaystyle\geq E_{\theta_{2}}\left[\lc(\theta_{1},\varphi(X))\exp\{-M(r_{2})^{\zeta}/\zeta\}\mathbf{1}\{X_{s_{n,2}}\leq M(r_{2})\}\right],

where we use Lemma
S-30
and that
M(r_{2})>0
.
From this one deduces that
\Sigma
is bounded from below by

\displaystyle\left\{\left(v_{C}(r_{1},\beta)^{-1}e^{-M(r_{2})^{\zeta}/\zeta}\right)\wedge v_{C}(r_{2},\beta)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \times E_{\theta_{2}}\left[\{\lc(\theta_{1},\varphi(X))+\lc(\theta_{2},\varphi(X))\}\mathbf{1}\{X_{s_{n,2}}\leq M(r_{2})\}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left\{\left(v_{C}(r_{1},\beta)^{-1}e^{-M(r_{2})^{\zeta}/\zeta}\right)\wedge v_{C}(r_{2},\beta)^{-1}\right\}\lc(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})P_{\theta_{2}}[X_{s_{n,2}}\leq M(r_{2})],

where we use the triangle inequality for the classification loss
\lc
. Noting that
\lc(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=1
and
P_{\theta_{2}}[X_{s_{n,2}}\leq M(r_{2})]=\Phi_{\zeta}(0)=1/2
, one obtains

2\Sigma\geq\left(v_{C}(r_{1},\beta)^{-1}n^{-r_{2}}\right)\wedge v_{C}(r_{2},\beta)^{-1}.

The result for the classification risk follows.

For the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk, let us denote by
L_{R}(\theta,\varphi)=\text{FDP}(\theta,\varphi)+\text{FNP}(\theta,\varphi)
the loss incurred by a testing procedure
\varphi
on a sparse vector
\theta
(the sum of the false discovery proportion and false negative proportion), so that
\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)=E_{\theta}L_{R}(\theta,\varphi)
. Similarly as above, one bounds

\Sigma_{R}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\varphi):=\frac{\mathfrak{R}(\theta_{1},\varphi)}{v_{R}(r_{1},\beta)}+\frac{\mathfrak{R}(\theta_{2},\varphi)}{v_{R}(r_{2},\beta)}

from below by, using the same argument,

\displaystyle\left\{\left(v_{R}(r_{1},\beta)^{-1}e^{-M(r_{2})^{\zeta}/\zeta}\right)\wedge v_{R}(r_{2},\beta)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \times E_{\theta_{2}}\left[\{L_{R}(\theta_{1},\varphi(X))+L_{R}(\theta_{2},\varphi(X))\}\mathbf{1}\{X_{s_{n,2}}\leq M(r_{2})\}\right].

The vectors
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
S-20
, which implies that
L_{R}(\theta_{1},\varphi(X))+L_{R}(\theta_{2},\varphi(X))
is at least
1/(s_{n,1}+2)
, regardless of
\varphi
. Using that
s_{n,1}\asymp n^{1-\beta}
and
n^{1-\beta}v_{R}(r_{i},\beta)=v_{C}(r_{i},\beta)
, the result follows.

Lemma S-20
.

Let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with respectively
|S_{\theta_{1}}|=s_{n}
and
|S_{\theta_{2}}|=s_{n}+1
nonzero coefficients, for some
1\leq s_{n}\leq n-1
. Suppose
|S_{\theta_{1}}\cap S_{\theta_{2}}|=s_{n}
. Then for any procedure
\varphi
,

\FDP(\theta_{1},\varphi)+\FNP(\theta_{1},\varphi)+\FDP(\theta_{2},\varphi)+\FNP(\theta_{2},\varphi)\geq\frac{1}{s_{n}+2}.

Proof.

For
\theta\in\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}
and
\varphi
arbitrary, by definition of the false discovery proportion, bounding from above the number of true discoveries by
|S_{\theta}|
,

\text{FDP}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\frac{N_{FP}(\theta,\varphi)}{N_{FP}(\theta,\varphi)+|S_{\theta}|}\geq\frac{N_{FP}(\theta,\varphi)}{N_{FP}(\theta,\varphi)+s_{n}+1}.

The last quantity is either
0
or bounded below by
1/(s_{n}+2)
, since
x\to x/(x+s_{n}+1)
is increasing for
x\geq 0
. Similarly,

\text{FNP}(\theta,\varphi)=\frac{N_{FN}(\theta,\varphi)}{|S_{\theta}|}\geq\frac{N_{FN}(\theta,\varphi)}{s_{n}+1},

which is either
0
or bounded below by
1/(s_{n}+1)
. Note that

\frac{N_{FP}(\theta_{1},\varphi)}{N_{FP}(\theta_{1},\varphi)+s_{n}+1}+\frac{N_{FN}(\theta_{1},\varphi)}{s_{n}+1}+\frac{N_{FP}(\theta_{2},\varphi)}{N_{FP}(\theta_{2},\varphi)+s_{n}+1}+\frac{N_{FN}(\theta_{2},\varphi)}{s_{n}+1}

cannot be zero. Indeed, if this was the case, this would mean that
N_{FP}(\theta_{1},\varphi)=N_{FN}(\theta_{1},\varphi)=0
that is
S_{\theta_{1}}=S_{\varphi}
but also
N_{FP}(\theta_{2},\varphi)=N_{FN}(\theta_{2},\varphi)=0
that is
S_{\theta_{2}}=S_{\varphi}
, so that
S_{\theta_{1}}=S_{\theta_{2}}
and this is impossible because
S_{\theta_{1}}\neq S_{\theta_{2}}
by definition. This implies that at least one of the four terms in the last display is nonzero, so the result follows, because individually all terms, if nonzero, are at least
1/(s_{n}+2)
.
∎

S-9.7
Proof of Theorem
S-8
[Thm
9
, (ii)]

Notation
N_{FP}(T,\theta),N_{FN}(T,\theta)
.
For a given procedure
T=T(X)
, in the proofs to follow for clarify we denote by
N_{FP}(T,\theta)
, respectively
N_{FN}(T,\theta)
, the number of false positives of
T
, resp. false negatives, if the true vector is
\theta
.

Proof.

We now give the proof of Theorem
S-8
. For simplicity denote
\varphi=\varphi[s_{n}]
. Both numbers
N_{FP}(T,\theta),N_{FN}(T,\theta)
are random variables taking values on the integers up to
n-s_{n}
(resp.
s_{n}
). Using that for such an integer–valued variable
N
it holds
E[N]=\sum_{k\geq 1}P[N\geq k]
, it is enough to bound the probabilities
P[N\geq k]
.

Let us denote by
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{s_{n}}
the random variables
\varepsilon_{i}
for
i\in S_{\theta}
(by this we mean
\eta_{i}=\varepsilon_{j_{i}}
for
j_{1}<\cdots<j_{s_{n}}
the indices in the support of
\theta
) and similarly by
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n-s_{n}}
the random variables
\varepsilon_{i}
for
i\notin S_{\theta}
.

First focusing on false positives, if the top–
s_{n}
procedure
\varphi
makes at least
k\geq 1
false positives, then among the
|\xi_{i}|
’s, at least
k
are at least
|X|_{(s_{n})}
. For any
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}[N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)\geq k]

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}[|\xi|_{(k)}\geq|X|_{(s_{n})}]

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}[|\xi|_{(k)}\geq\tau]+P_{\theta}[|\xi|_{(k)}\geq|X|_{(s_{n})}\,,\,|\xi|_{(k)}<\tau]

By independence of
\xi_{i}
’s, one has
P_{\theta}[|\xi|_{(k)}\geq\tau]=P_{\theta}[\text{Bin}(n-s_{n},2\bar{\Phi}(\tau))\geq k]
. On the other hand, if
\tau>|\xi|_{(k)}\geq|X|_{(s_{n})}
, then at least
k
among the variables
|\theta_{i}+\eta_{i}|
are less or equal to
|\xi|_{(k)}<\tau
. Denoting by
p_{i}(\tau)=p_{i}(\tau,\theta):=P_{\theta}[|X_{i}|\leq\tau]
for
i\in S_{\theta}
and renumbering
p_{1}^{*}(\tau),\ldots,p_{s_{n}}^{*}(\tau)
the successive values of the
p_{i}(\tau)
’s,

\displaystyle P_{\theta}[N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)\geq k]

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta}[\text{Bin}(n-s_{n},2\bar{\Phi}(\tau))\geq k]+P_{\theta}[\text{PBin}(s_{n},(p_{j}^{*}(\tau)_{j}))\geq k].

Deduce, using the formula for the expectation once again, that

\displaystyle E_{\theta}N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\leq E[\text{Bin}(n-s_{n},2\bar{\Phi}(\tau))]+E[\text{PBin}(s_{n},(p_{j}^{*}(\tau)_{j}))]

\displaystyle=E_{\theta}\left[N_{FP}(T(\tau),\theta)+N_{FN}(T(\tau),\theta)\right]=E_{\theta}\lc(T(\tau),\theta),

where
T(\tau)(X)_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|\geq\tau\}
is the thresholding procedure at level
\tau
.

Turning now to the number of false negatives of
\varphi
, one notes that if
\varphi
makes at least
k\geq 1
false negatives, then among the
|X_{i}|
’s for
i\in S_{\theta}
, at least
k
are below
|X|_{(s_{n})}
. This means that at least
k
among the noise variables
|\xi|_{i}
’s are above
|X|_{(s_{n})}
which means that
P_{\theta}[N_{FN}(\varphi,\theta)\geq k]\leq P_{\theta}[|\xi|_{(k)}\geq|X|_{(s_{n})}]
and this quantity has already been bounded before (one may note that this reasoning also gives
N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)=N_{FN}(\varphi,\theta)
). The same reasoning then gives that
E_{\theta}N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)\leq E_{\theta}\lc(T(\tau),\theta)
. By taking the infimum over
\tau>0
, this concludes the proof.
∎

S-9.8
Proof of Corollary
S-3

Proof.

First note that for the procedure
\varphi
one has the identity
E_{\theta}\lc(\varphi,\theta)=s_{n}\mathfrak{R}(\varphi,\theta)
for all
\theta
since
\varphi
rejects exactly
s_{n}
coordinates (i.e. there are no ties among
X_{i}
’s) with probability
1
.
Focusing on the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk, the corresponding supremum in the statement is bounded from above, using Theorem
S-8
, by
2\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\inf_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathfrak{R}(T,\theta)
. This is in turn bounded by twice the maximum normalised risk of the oracle thresholding procedure with threshold
\sqrt{2\log{n/s_{n}}}
. By Theorem
1
, this is
2\bar{\Phi}(b)+o(1)
.

To obtain a matching lower bound, note that
\mathfrak{R}(\varphi,\theta)=E_{\theta}[N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)+N_{FN}(\varphi,\theta)]/s_{n}=2E_{\theta}[N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)]/s_{n}=2\FNR(\varphi,\theta)
, where we have used that
N_{FP}(\varphi,\theta)=N_{FN}(\varphi,\theta)
as noted in the proof of Theorem
S-8
. The supremum in the statement is thus bounded below by
2\inf_{T}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}\FNR(T,\theta)
. Since
\varphi
is sparsity–preserving, Theorem
3
in the main paper implies that the latter bound is at least
2\bar{\Phi}(b)+o(1)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

S-9.9
Proof of Theorem
S-9
[Thm
9
, (iii)]

Proof.

Let us recall the notation
\tau(r,\beta)=\{\sqrt{r}/2+\beta/(2\sqrt{r})\}\sqrt{2\log{n}}
. Below we use that this threshold verifies for
s_{n}\asymp n^{1-\beta}
and
v_{C}(r,\beta)=v_{C}(r,\beta,2)
the classification rate for Gaussian noise,

(n-s_{n})\overline{\Phi}(\tau(r,\beta))+s_{n}\overline{\Phi}(\sqrt{2r\log(n)})-\tau(r,\beta)\asymp v_{C}(r,\beta)

and denote, recalling that the constant
a
arises in the definition of the class
\Theta(r,\beta)
in (
32
),

S_{n}=an^{1-\beta}/\log{n}

as shorthand notation. Lemma
S-21
below tells us that for some
C_{1},C_{2}
, writing
\beta_{\pm}=\beta\pm C_{1}/\log^{2}n
, we have
\hat{\beta}\in[\beta_{-},\beta_{+}]
on an event
\mathcal{C}
of probability at least
1-e^{-C_{2}S_{n}}
.
Let us denote by
\varphi^{+}
and
\varphi^{-}
the thresholding procedures with thresholds
\tau(r,\beta^{+})
and
\tau(r,\beta^{-})
respectively.
By monotonicity,
N_{FP}(\varphi^{r},\theta)\leq N_{FP}(\varphi^{-},\theta)
and
N_{FN}(\varphi^{r},\theta)\leq N_{FN}(\varphi^{+},\theta)
on the event
\mathcal{C}
. Since
N_{FP}\leq n
, deduce, for any
\theta
in
\Theta(r,\beta)
with
|S_{\theta}|=s_{n}
,

\displaystyle E_{\theta}N_{FP}(\varphi^{r},\theta)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\theta}[N_{FP}(\varphi^{-},\theta)1_{\mathcal{C}}]+nP_{\theta}[\mathcal{C}^{c}]

\displaystyle\leq E_{\theta}[\text{Bin}(n-s_{n},2\bar{\Phi}(\tau(r,\beta^{-})))]+ne^{-C_{2}S_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(n-S_{n})\bar{\Phi}(\tau(r,\beta^{-}))+ne^{-C_{2}S_{n}}.

Similarly, if
i_{1},\ldots,i_{s_{n}}
denote the indices in the support of
\theta
, for PBin
(\bm{a})
the Poisson-Binomial distribution (Section
S-10
) with vector of parameters
\bm{a}
,

\displaystyle E_{\theta}N_{FN}(\varphi^{r},\theta)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\theta}[\text{PBin}(\{P(|\theta_{i_{j}}+\varepsilon_{i_{j}}|\leq\tau(r,\beta^{+}))\}_{1\leq j\leq s_{n}})]+ne^{-C_{2}S_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq s_{n}\bar{\Phi}(\sqrt{2r\log{n}}-\tau(r,\beta^{+}))+ne^{-C_{2}S_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq(bS_{n}/a)\bar{\Phi}(\sqrt{2r\log{n}}-\tau(r,\beta^{+}))+ne^{-C_{2}S_{n}}.

Deduce, using Lemma
S-24
, since
\tau(r,\beta^{+})
and
\tau(r,\beta^{-})
are only of order
\log^{-3/2}(n)
away from
\tau(r,\beta)
, that replacing the later quantities by
\tau(r,\beta)
in the previous bounds only leads to multiplicative factors that are
1+O(1)
as
n\to\infty
. So

E_{\theta}\lc(\varphi^{r},\theta)\leq\{2(n-S_{n})\bar{\Phi}(\tau(r,\beta))+\frac{bS_{n}}{a}\bar{\Phi}(\sqrt{2r\log{n}}-\tau(r,\beta))\}(1+O(1))+2ne^{-C_{2}S_{n}}.

As the first term in the last bound is of order
v_{C}(r,\beta)
(which decays at most at a polynomial rate in
n
) and the last one is exponentially small in
n
, the result for
\lc
follows.

The result for the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk follows quite easily. Let us briefly sketch the argument. It is enough to show that with overwhelming probability,
N_{FN}(\varphi^{r},\theta)
is no more than, say,
s_{n}/2
over
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
. Indeed, on the corresponding event, we must then have
\FDP(\varphi^{r},\theta)\leq 2\lc(\varphi^{r},\theta)/s_{n}
and one concludes by taking expectations. To show the claimed high probability bound on
N_{FN}(\varphi^{r},\theta)
, it suffices to note that
\varphi^{r}
thresholds by definition at
\tau(\hat{\beta},r)
, which is less than
\sqrt{2r_{1}\log{n}}
for some
r_{1}<r
with overwhelming probability (for
n\geq N(\varepsilon)
large enough) thanks to Lemma
S-21
and noting that
\beta\to\tau(r,\beta)
is away from
r
for
\beta
away from
r
. Now it suffices to note that the probability that
N_{FN}(\varphi^{r},\theta)>s_{n}/2
is binomial with success probability strictly less than
1/4
(say), which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma S-21
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. In the setting of Theorem
S-9
, there exists an estimator
\hat{\beta}
and
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for
n\geq N(\varepsilon)
large enough,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}P_{\theta}\left[|\hat{\beta}-\beta|>C_{1}/\log^{2}{n}\right]\leq e^{-C_{2}an^{1-\beta}/\log{n}}.

Proof of Lemma
S-21
.

We consider the
\ell
-value procedure from
[
19
]
, see also Section
S-8.2
(one could also use a BH procedure): recall that this procedure is defined as

\varphi^{\ell}(X_{i})=\varphi^{\ell}_{\hat{w}}(X_{i})=1\{\ell(X_{i};\hat{w},g)\leq t\},

(S-59)

for some fixed small
t
, say
t=1/4
, and
\ell(x;w,g)=(1-w)\phi(x)/\{(1-w)\phi(x)+wg(x)\}
, where we take the quasi-Cauchy density
g
and where
\hat{w}
is the marginal maximum likelihood estimator for
w
.

We first derive the bound in the Lemma for
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
with
|S_{\theta}|=s_{n}
.
By Lemmas
S-10
and
S-11
, (noting that the set of large signals considered here is a subset of the signals considered for these Lemmas), if
\mathcal{B}=\{\hat{w}\in[w_{-},w_{+}]\}
,

P_{\theta}[\mathcal{B}^{c}]\leq 2e^{-Cs_{n}},

as well as
w_{-}\asymp w_{+}\asymp(s_{n}/n)\tilde{m}(n/s_{n})^{-1}\asymp(s_{n}/n)\sqrt{\log(n/s_{n})}
. It follows, using

\varphi_{\hat{w}}^{\ell}(X_{i})\geq\varphi_{w_{2}}^{\ell}(X_{i})

by monotonicity (Lemma
S-13
) that
\varphi^{\ell}
equals
1
on
X_{i}
’s larger than
\xi(r(w_{+},t))
. But

\xi(r(w_{+},t))\leq\sqrt{2\beta\log{n}}+o(1)=M(\beta)+o(1)

by Lemma
S-19
. This bound in turn is smaller than
M(\beta+\varepsilon/2)
for
n\geq N(\varepsilon)
. Now recall that signals are at least
M(r)
by definition of the class and that we assume
r\geq\beta+\varepsilon
. Bernstein’s inequality now gives that there are at most
s_{n}/\log{s_{n}}
signals that are below
M(\beta+\varepsilon/2)
with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cs_{n}/\log{s_{n}})
.
Deduce that if
N_{FN}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta)
is the number of false negatives of the procedure
\varphi^{\ell}
then with
K_{n}=s_{n}/\log{s_{n}}\gtrsim s_{n}/\log{n}
,

P_{\theta}\left[N_{FN}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta)>K_{n}\right]\leq\exp\{-CK_{n}\}.

Let us now turn to the number
N_{FP}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta)
of false positives of the procedure
\varphi^{\ell}
. On the event
\mathcal{B}
, we have
\varphi_{\hat{w}}^{\ell}(X_{i})\leq\varphi_{w_{-}}^{\ell}(X_{i})
by monotonicity (Lemma
S-13
) so
N_{FP}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta)\leq N_{FP}(\varphi^{\ell}_{w_{-}},\theta)
on
\mathcal{B}
. On the other hand,
N_{FP}(\varphi^{\ell}_{w_{-}},\theta)
equals in distribution a
\text{Bin}(n-s_{n},2\bar{\Phi}(\xi(r(w_{-},t))))
. Using that

\bar{\Phi}(\xi(r(w_{-},t)))\asymp t(s_{n}/n)\zeta(w_{-})^{-2},

and combining this with Bernstein’s inequality one gets, with
K_{n}^{\prime}=s_{n}/\zeta(w_{-})^{2}
, for suitable constants
c,C>0
,

P_{\theta}\left[N_{FP}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta)>cK_{n}^{\prime}\right]\leq\exp\{-CK_{n}^{\prime}\}.

Note that
K_{n}\asymp K_{n}^{\prime}\asymp s_{n}/\log{n}
and let us set

\hat{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi^{\ell}(X_{i}).

The above bounds on
N_{FN}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta),N_{FP}(\varphi^{\ell},\theta)
imply

P_{\theta}\left[|\hat{s}-s_{n}|>C_{1}s_{n}/\log{n}\right]\leq e^{-Cs_{n}/\log{n}}\leq e^{-C^{\prime}S_{n}}.

Setting
\hat{\beta}:=\log(n/\hat{s})/\log{n}
, for
\hat{s}
as above, one gets

P_{\theta}\left[|\hat{\beta}-\beta|>C_{1}/\log^{2}{n}\right]\leq e^{-C_{2}S_{n}}.

This bound is valid for any
\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)
with
|S_{\theta}|=s_{n}
. Since the bound is independent on
s_{n}
, it is also valid over
\Theta(r,\beta)
, which yields the result. ∎

S-9.10
Proof of Theorem
S-10
[Thm
9
, (iv)]

Proof.

Let us consider the family of thresholding estimates
T(a)
for some
a>0
defined as
T(a)(X)_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|>M(a)\}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, where
M(a)=\sqrt{2a\log{n}}
. A bound for the classification risk of
T(a)
is recalled in Lemma
S-22
.

We define an estimator using Lepski’s method.
One defines the grid, for
\delta_{n}=1/\log{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{n}

\displaystyle=\left\{\delta_{n},2\delta_{n},\ldots,\lfloor 4/\delta_{n}\rfloor\delta_{n}\right\},=\left\{a_{k}:=k\delta_{n},\ k\in\mathcal{K}_{n}\right\},

with
\mathcal{K}_{n}=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor 4/\delta_{n}\rfloor\}
.

For the ‘true’ signal strength
r
and any
l\in\mathcal{K}_{n}
, let us set

v_{l}=2n\bar{\Phi}\left(\sqrt{a_{l}}\sqrt{2\log{n}}\right),\qquad B(l)=2bn^{1-\beta}\left\{\bar{\Phi}\left(\{\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{a_{l}}\}\sqrt{2\log{n}}\right)\wedge 1\right\}.

(S-60)

Define, for
L=8
,

\hat{k}=\max\left\{k\in\mathcal{K}_{n},\ \lc(T(a_{k}),T(a_{l}))\leq Lv_{l},\ \text{for all }\ l\leq k\right\},

(S-61)

Also define, for
B(k),v_{k}
as in (
S-60
), and
\mathcal{K}_{n}
as above,

\displaystyle k^{*}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{k\in\mathcal{K}_{n},\ \ B(k)\leq v_{k}\right\}.

(S-62)

The estimator is defined, for
\hat{k}
as in (
S-61
),
as

\varphi(X)=T(a_{\hat{k}}).

One bounds the classification risk of
\varphi
for
\theta_{0}\in\Theta(r,\beta)
by

E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(\varphi,\theta_{0})\leq E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(T(a_{\hat{k}}),T(a_{k^{*}}))+E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(T(a_{k^{*}}),\theta_{0}).

Lemma
S-22
now gives

E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(T(a_{k^{*}}),\theta_{0})\leq B(k^{*})+v_{k^{*}}\leq 2v_{k^{*}}
,
while by the triangle inequality the other term is bounded by

E_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{\hat{k}}),T(a_{k^{*}}))\mathbf{1}\{\hat{k}<k^{*}\}]+E_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{\hat{k}}),T(a_{k^{*}}))\mathbf{1}\{\hat{k}>k^{*}\}]=:(i)+(ii).

Dealing first with the term (ii), on the event
\{\hat{k}>k^{*}\}
, by definition

\lc(T(a_{\hat{k}}),T(a_{k^{*}}))\leq Lv_{k^{*}}
. To bound (i), one splits

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k^{*}-1}E_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{p}),T(a_{k^{*}}))\mathbf{1}\{\hat{k}=p\}]\leq n\sum_{p=0}^{k^{*}-1}P_{\theta_{0}}[\hat{k}=p],

using the rough bound
\lc(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\leq n
. Using the definition of
\hat{k}
once again, since
p\leq k^{*}-1
,

\displaystyle P_{\theta_{0}}[\hat{k}=p]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{k^{*}-1}P_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{k^{*}}),T(a_{l}))>Lv_{l}]

\displaystyle\leq 2k^{*}\max_{0\leq l\leq k^{*}}P_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})>Lv_{l}/2],

bounding the sum by
k^{*}
times the maximum and using the triangle inequality. By Lemma
S-23
,

(i)\leq 4n(k^{*})^{2}e^{-v_{k^{*}}/4},

using that for
l\leq k^{*}
, one has
v_{l}\geq v_{k^{*}}
. We have obtained, for any
\theta_{0}\in\Theta(r,\beta)
,

E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(\varphi,\theta_{0})\leq(2+L)v_{k^{*}}+4n(k^{*})^{2}e^{-v_{k^{*}}/4}.

To conclude it is enough to verify that, for
(\beta,r)\in\mathcal{J}
, we (uniformly) have
v_{k^{*}}\asymp v_{C}(r,\beta)
and that
v_{C}(r,\beta)
is larger than a small power of
n
, so that the last term in the above display is indeed a
o(v_{k^{*}})
. Define
a_{l^{*}}
to be the (or ‘a’ in case there are two) closest point on the grid
\mathcal{A}_{n}
to
a^{*}:=\beta+\kappa(r,\beta)
. Then

v_{l^{*}}\sim\frac{2n^{1-a_{l^{*}}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sqrt{2a_{l^{*}}\log{n}}}\sim\frac{2n^{1-a^{*}+(a^{*}-a_{l^{*}})}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sqrt{2a_{l^{*}}\log{n}}}\asymp\frac{n^{1-a^{*}+O(\delta_{n})}}{\sqrt{\log{n}}},

and

B(l^{*})\sim\frac{2bn^{1-\beta}n^{-(\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{a_{l^{*}}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sqrt{2(\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{a_{l^{*}}})\log{n}}}\asymp\frac{n^{1-\beta-(\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{a^{*}})^{2}+O(\delta_{n})}}{\sqrt{\log{n}}}.

One checks using the definition of
a^{*}
that
a^{*}=\beta+(\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{a^{*}})^{2}
which shows that
v_{l^{*}}\asymp B_{l^{*}}
. Since for finite
K
we have
v_{l^{*}\pm K}\sim e^{\mp K}v_{l^{*}}
and since
l\to B(l)
is increasing, we deduce
|l^{*}-k^{*}|
is finite so that
v_{k^{*}}
is of the same order as
v_{l^{*}}\asymp n^{1-\beta-\kappa(r,\beta)}/\sqrt{\log{n}}\asymp v_{C}(r,\beta)
. This concludes the proof for the classification risk.

To deduce the result for the
\mathfrak{R}
–risk, one uses a similar argument as in the proof of Theorem
S-9
: as the threshold is in the large signal regime, the denominator of the FDP is of order
s_{n}
with high probability. Details are the same as in the earlier proof and are omitted. ∎

Lemma S-22
.

Let
T(a)
for some
a>0
be defined as
T(a)(X)_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|>M(a)\}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, where
M(a)=\sqrt{2a\log{n}}
. Then, for
1\leq s_{n}\leq n
, and any
\theta
with
|S_{\theta}|=s_{n}
and
|\theta_{i}|\geq M(r)
for
i\in S_{\theta}
,

E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,T(a))\leq 2(n-s_{n})\bar{\Phi}\left(\sqrt{a}\sqrt{2\log{n}}\right)+s_{n}\{2\bar{\Phi}\left((\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{a})\sqrt{2\log{n}}\right)\wedge 1\}.

Proof.

For any
\theta
as in the statement of the lemma, we have

\displaystyle E_{\theta}\lc(T(a),\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\notin S_{\theta}}P_{\theta}[|X_{i}|>M(a)]+\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}P_{\theta}[|X_{i}|\leq M(a)].

If
\theta\in\ell_{0}[s_{n}]\setminus\ell_{0}[s_{n}-1]
, for fixed
a>0
,
the first sum equals
2(n-s_{n})\bar{\Phi}(M(a))
.
On the other hand, if
\theta\in\ell[s_{n}]\setminus\ell[s_{n}-1]
, for fixed
a>0
, the second sum in the last display is

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}P_{\theta}(|X_{i}|<M(a))=\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\{\Phi(M(a)-\theta_{i})-\Phi(-M(a)-\theta_{i})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in S_{\theta}}\{2\bar{\Phi}(|\theta_{i}|-M(a))\wedge 1\}.

To conclude one uses the condition on nonzero signals and that
\bar{\Phi}
is decreasing.
∎

Lemma S-23
.

Let
v_{l}
be defined by (
S-60
) and
k^{*}
by (
S-62
). For any
\theta_{0}\in\Theta(r,\beta)
and any
l\leq k^{*}
, one has

P_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})>4v_{l}]\leq 2e^{-v_{l}/4}.

Proof.

Note
E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})\leq B(l)+v_{l}\leq B(k^{*})+v_{l}\leq v_{k^{*}}+v_{l}\leq 2v_{l}
using
l\leq k^{*}
and that
l\to B(l)
(respectively
l\to v_{l}
) is increasing (respectively decreasing). So

\displaystyle P_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})>4v_{l}]

\displaystyle=P_{\theta_{0}}[\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})-E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})>4v_{l}-E_{\theta_{0}}\lc(T(a_{l}),\theta_{0})]

\displaystyle\leq P_{\theta_{0}}\Big[\sum_{i\notin S_{\theta_{0}}}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|>M(a_{l})\}-P_{\theta_{0}}(|X_{i}|>M(a_{l}))\right\}>v_{l}\Big]

\displaystyle\ \ +P_{\theta_{0}}\Big[\sum_{i\in S_{\theta_{0}}}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|\leq M(a_{l})\}-P_{\theta_{0}}(|X_{i}|\leq M(a_{l}))\right\}>v_{l}\Big].

Applying Bernstein’s inequality to each term in the previous expression concludes the proof, noting that the sum of variances for the first term is bounded by
v_{l}
and for the second term by
B(l)\leq v_{l}
when
l\leq k^{*}
.
∎

Lemma S-24
.

In the setting of Lemma
S-22
, for
\kappa
as in (
S-51
), let
\tau
be such that, for
(R_{n})
any sequence with
R_{n}^{2}=o(\log{n})
,

\tau=\sqrt{2\log{n}}\left(\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{\kappa}\right)+o(R_{n}).

Then the thresholding procedure
\varphi_{\tau}(X)_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{|X_{i}|>\tau\}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, satisfies

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}E_{\theta}\lc(\theta,\varphi_{\tau})\lesssim n^{1-\beta-\kappa}/\sqrt{\log{n}}.

Proof.

One applies Lemma
S-22
with
\sqrt{a}=\sqrt{r}-\sqrt{\kappa}+R_{n}/\sqrt{2\log{n}}
. Using the bound
\bar{\Phi}(x)\leq\phi(x)/x
for
x>0
, one bounds from above each of the terms appearing in Lemma
S-22
by
O(n^{1-\beta-\kappa})/\sqrt{\log{n}}
, which gives the result.
∎

S-10
The Poisson-binomial distribution and concentration inequalities

Let
\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{a}]
denote the Poisson-binomial distribution of parameter
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{S})
, for
S\geq 1
: it is the distribution of
\sum_{i=1}^{S}Z_{i}
, where
Z_{i}
are independent
\text{Be}(a_{i})
random variables.

Lemma S-25
.

Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{S}),\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{S})
be vectors in
[0,1]^{S}
for some
S\geq 1
. Suppose
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq S
. Then, for any integer
k
,

P[\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{a}]\geq k]\leq P[\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{b}]\geq k].

That is,
\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{b}]
stochastically dominates
\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{a}]
.

Proof.

By transitivity it is enough to prove the result in the case
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}
differ only by one coordinate, say (by symmetry) the first one: that is, let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{S})
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{S})
. If
X_{1}\sim\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{a}]
and
X_{2}\sim\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{b}]
, one can write
X_{1}=\varepsilon+T_{1}
and
X_{2}=\xi+T_{2}
(in distribution), where
T_{1},T_{2}
are equal in law, and
\varepsilon\sim\operatorname{Be}(a_{1}),\xi\sim\operatorname{Be}(b_{1})
, independently of
T_{1},T_{2}
respectively. For any integer
k
,

\displaystyle P[X_{1}\geq k]

\displaystyle=P[T_{1}+1\geq k\,|\,\varepsilon=1]a_{1}+P[T_{1}\geq k\,|\,\varepsilon=0](1-a_{1})

\displaystyle\leq P[T_{1}+1\geq k\,|\,\varepsilon=1]b_{1}+P[T_{1}\geq k\,|\,\varepsilon=0](1-b_{1})=P[X_{2}\geq k],

where the second line uses that
P[T_{1}+1\geq k\,|\,\varepsilon=1]-P[T_{1}\geq k\,|\,\varepsilon=0]=P[T_{1}\geq k-1]-P[T_{1}\geq k]\geq 0
and
a_{1}\leq b_{1}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma S-26
.

Let
\mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{S})
be a vector of independent random variables taking values in
[0,1]
, and let
\bm{p}=(p_{j})\in[0,1]^{S}
be a deterministic vector such that
E[Y_{j}]=p_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq S
.
Conditionally on
\bm{Y}
, let
\xi_{j}\sim\operatorname{Be}(Y_{j})
,
j\leq S
be independent Bernoulli random variables. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{S}\xi_{j}\sim\operatorname{PBin}[\bm{p}].

Proof.

Observe that the variables
\xi_{j}
, defined to be independent given
\mathbf{Y}
, are also independent unconditionally, because the
Y_{j}
’s are independent. It now suffices to note that
\xi_{j}
has Bernoulli distribution, with parameter
E[\xi_{j}]=E[E[\xi_{j}\,|\,Y_{j}]]=E[Y_{j}]=p_{j}
.
∎

Lemma S-27

(Bernstein’s inequality)
.

Let
W_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
centered independent variables with
|W_{i}|\leq\mathcal{M}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Var(W_{i})\leq V
, then for any
A>0
,

P\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}>A\right]\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}A^{2}/(V+\mathcal{M}A/3)\right\}.

In particular, let
\mathcal{P}\sim\operatorname{PBin}[\bm{p}]
, with
\bm{p}\in[0,1]^{S}
,
S\geq 1
, and set
\mu:=\sum_{j=1}^{S}p_{j}
. Then for any
0\leq\delta\leq 1
,

P[\mathcal{P}\geq\mu(1+\delta)]\leq e^{-\mu\delta^{2}/3}.

Proof.

The first part of the lemma is the standard Bernstein inequality. The second part follows, for
\xi_{j}
independent Bernoulli variables with parameter
p_{j}
, by setting
W_{j}=\xi_{j}-E[\xi_{j}],A=\mu\delta,V=\sum_{j=1}^{S}p_{j}=\mu,\mathcal{M}=1
and noting, since
0\leq\delta\leq 1
,

\exp(-\frac{\mu^{2}\delta^{2}}{2\mu+2\mu\delta/3})\leq\exp(-3\mu\delta^{2}/8)\leq\exp(-\mu\delta^{2}/3).\qed

S-11
Properties of Subbotin distributions

Lemma S-28
.

Let
\phi_{\zeta}(\cdot)
be defined by (
20
) for some
\zeta>1
. Then
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)=\int_{x}^{+\infty}\phi_{\zeta}(u)du
has the following properties:

•

for any
x>0
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle<\phi_{\zeta}(x)/x^{\zeta-1}\,;

(S-63)

•

for any
t\in(0,1/2)
s.t.
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq(\zeta\log(1/t)-\zeta\log L_{\zeta})^{1/\zeta}\,;

(S-64)

The following holds:

•

for any
x>0
,

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi_{\zeta}(x)}{x^{\zeta-1}}\bigg[1+(\zeta-1)x^{-\zeta}\bigg]^{-1}\,;

(S-65)

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi_{\zeta}(x)}{x^{\zeta-1}}\zeta^{-1}\>\mbox{ if $x\geq 1$}\,;

(S-66)

•

for any
t\in(0,1/2)
s.t.
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\left(0\vee\bigg\{\zeta\log(1/t)-\zeta\log(\zeta L_{\zeta})-(\zeta-1)\log\left(\zeta\log(1/t)-\zeta\log L_{\zeta}\right)\bigg\}\right)^{1/\zeta}.

(S-67)

Proof.

Let
\psi(u)=u^{\zeta}/\zeta+\log L_{\zeta}
, so that, restricting to
u>0
,

\phi_{\zeta}(u)=e^{-\psi(u)},\quad\psi^{\prime}(u)=u^{\zeta-1},\quad\psi^{-1}(v)=(\zeta v-\zeta\log L_{\zeta})^{1/\zeta},\quad\psi^{\prime\prime}(u)=(\zeta-1)u^{\zeta-2},

and thus for instance
\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi^{-1}(v)=(\zeta v-\zeta\log L_{\zeta})^{1-1/\zeta}
,
\psi^{\prime\prime}(u)/(\psi^{\prime}(u))^{2}=(\zeta-1)u^{-\zeta}
.
Inequality (
S-63
) holds because
\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)=\int_{x}^{+\infty}e^{-\psi(u)}du<(\psi^{\prime}(x))^{-1}\int_{x}^{+\infty}\psi^{\prime}(u)e^{-\psi(u)}du=\phi_{\zeta}(x)/\psi^{\prime}(x)
. Expression (
S-64
) follows from (
S-63
) applied with
x=\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)\geq 1
. Indeed, the latter entails
L_{\zeta}^{-1}\exp(-\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)^{\zeta}/\zeta)>tx^{\zeta-1}\geq t
, from which (
S-64
) follows by applying the function
\log(\cdot)
to both sides of the inequality.
To prove (
S-65
), write for any
x>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(x)}{\psi^{\prime}(x)^{2}}\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{x}^{+\infty}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(u)}{\psi^{\prime}(u)^{2}}e^{-\psi(u)}du=\bigg[-\frac{e^{-\psi(u)}}{\psi^{\prime}(u)}\bigg]_{x}^{\infty}-\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x)=\frac{\phi_{\zeta}(x)}{\psi^{\prime}(x)}-\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}(x),

by using an integration by parts. Expressions (
S-65
) and (
S-66
) follow.
Finally, let us prove (
S-67
). From (
S-66
) used with
x=\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t)
, we get
\zeta t(\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t))^{\zeta-1}\geq e^{-\psi(\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t))}
and thus
-\log(\zeta t)-(\zeta-1)\log(\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t))\leq\psi(\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t))
. Hence, by (
S-65
), we obtain

\log L_{\zeta}\vee\left(-\log(\zeta t)-(1-1/\zeta)\log(\zeta\log(1/t)-\zeta\log L_{\zeta})\right)\leq\psi(\overline{\Phi}_{\zeta}^{-1}(t))

from which (
S-67
) follows.
∎

Lemma S-29
.

For any
a\geq 0
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\zeta}(x-a)/\phi_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\big(a\lvert x-a\rvert^{\zeta-1}\sign(x-a)\big),

\displaystyle\phi_{\zeta}(x+a)/\phi_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big(-a\lvert x\rvert^{\zeta-1}\sign(x)\big).

Proof.

Since
\zeta>1
, observe that
-\log\phi_{\zeta}(x)=\zeta^{-1}\lvert x\rvert^{\zeta}+c
is differentiable (even at
0
) with increasing derivative
\lvert x\rvert^{\zeta-1}\sign(x)
. It follows, using
a\geq 0
, that

-\log\phi_{\zeta}(x)\geq-\log\phi_{\zeta}(x-a)+a\lvert x-a\rvert^{\zeta-1}\sign(x-a)
, and hence the first claim. The second follows by substituting
x+a
for
x
.
∎

Lemma S-30
.

Let
\zeta>1
. For any
a\geq 0
and any
x\leq a
,

\phi_{\zeta}(x)/\phi_{\zeta}(x-a)\geq\exp\big(-a^{\zeta}/\zeta\big).

Proof.

The ratio in the statement is
\exp(-|x|^{\zeta}/\zeta+(a-x)^{\zeta}/\zeta)
by definition for
x\leq a
. The map
x\to(a-x)^{\zeta}-|x|^{\zeta}
is decreasing on
(-\infty,a]
: indeed, its derivative for
x\neq 0
is
-\zeta(a-x)^{\zeta-1}-\zeta\sign(x)|x|^{\zeta-1}
which is negative for
x\neq 0
, using that
u\to u^{\zeta-1}
is increasing on
[0,+\infty)
since
\zeta>1
.
∎

S-12
In-probability classification risk bounds

In this section we give several in-probability bounds for the classification risk, both for general signals and in the large signal case for classes
\Theta(r,\beta)
as in (
32
). Upper bounds have been given already for the
\ell
-value and BH procedures, hence the focus here is on proving in-probability lower bounds. These results are interesting on their own, and in the general signal case they can also be used to provide an alternative proof of the lower bound part of the main Theorem
4
for general signals.

S-12.1
Classification: sharp adaptive minimaxity in probability

Recall the definition of the classification loss

\lc(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}\neq 0\}+\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}=0\}\right).

Theorem S-11
.

If
a_{b}=\sqrt{2\log{n/s_{n}}}+b
for a fixed real
b
, then for
\Theta_{b}=\Theta(a_{b};s_{n})
and any
\eta>0
,

{\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}\geq\overline{\Phi}(b)-\eta\right]=1+o(1).}

If
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
, the last display holds with
\overline{\Phi}(b)
replaced by 1.

There exist procedures
\varphi
achieving this bound, in that for any fixed real
b
and any
\eta>0

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{b}}P_{\theta}\left[\lc(\theta,\varphi)/s_{n}\geq\overline{\Phi}(b)+\eta\right]=o(1),

and the same holds with
\overline{\Phi}(b)
replaced by 0 when
b=b_{n}\to+\infty
or by 1 when
b=b_{n}\to-\infty
.
In particular this is true for
\varphi=\varphi^{\hat{\ell}}
the empirical Bayes
\ell
-value procedure (
S-29
) or, under the polynomial sparsity assumption (
12
), the BH procedure
\varphi^{BH}_{\alpha}
(
S-18
) for
\alpha=\alpha_{n}=o(1)
with
-\log\alpha=o((\log n)^{1/2})
.

The upper bounds for the BH procedure and
\ell
-value procedure can be found in Sections
S-7.3
and
S-8.3
respectively. The lower bound of Theorem
S-11
follows from Theorem
S-13
(to follow), which gives a corresponding bound over the more general set
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
of (
21
). Specifically, by taking
\rho=1
, it says that for
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
and
n
large enough that
s_{n}>4
, we have

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}P_{\theta}\Big(\lc(\theta,\varphi)\geq\min\big(1-\tfrac{1+\varepsilon}{\sqrt{s_{n}}},(\Lambda_{n}-\varepsilon)\big)s_{n}\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-\sqrt{s_{n}}\varepsilon^{2}/3).

Since
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})=\Theta_{b}
if
\bm{a}=(a_{n}^{*}+b,\dots,a_{n}^{*}+b)
, and in the Gaussian model
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})=\overline{\Phi}(b)
for this
\bm{a}
, we deduce the claim.

S-12.2
Classification and large signals, in probability results

The following result can be proved using similar arguments as Theorem
S-11
, adapting these to the large signals regime. We state it in Gaussian noise for simplicity and omit the proof.

Theorem S-12
.

Consider the same setting as Theorem
8
, in the Gaussian noise case
\zeta=2
and with
\beta\in(0,1)
and
r>\beta
.

If
n^{1-\beta-\kappa}/\sqrt{\log{n}}
is bounded away from zero, for
M_{n}
going to
\infty
arbitrarily slowly,

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}P_{\theta}\left(\lc(\theta,\varphi)\geq M_{n}n^{1-\beta-\kappa}/\sqrt{\log{n}}\right)\to 1,\quad(n\to\infty).

If on the other hand
n^{1-\beta-\kappa}/\sqrt{\log{n}}
is bounded from above, it holds

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(r,\beta)}P_{\theta}\left(\lc(\theta,\varphi)\neq 0\right)\asymp n^{1-\beta-\kappa}/\sqrt{\log{n}},\quad(n\to\infty).

Aside from proving an in-probability lower bound that matches the in-expectation bound from Theorem
8
in the first part of the statement, we quantify the vanishing probability of the existence of at least one misclassified label in the ‘exact recovery’ regime in the second part of the statement.

S-12.3
Classification: lower bounds for weighted loss

Before proving the main result of this section, Theorem
S-13
, let us show briefly how this result can be used to provide an alternative proof of Theorem
4
(which is an ‘in-expectation’ result).
Recall from (
S-9
) the definition for
\rho>0
of a weighted, non-symmetric loss function

L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}\neq 0\}+\rho\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}=0\}\right\}.

(S-68)

Standard classification loss
\lc
corresponds to
\rho=1
. For
\varepsilon>0
, we may apply Theorem
S-13
with a sequence
\rho=\rho_{n}\to\infty
to obtain

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}P_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\Big(L_{\rho}(\theta,T)\geq\min\Big(1-\tfrac{1+\varepsilon}{\sqrt{s_{n}}},\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-\varepsilon\Big)\rho s_{n}\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-\sqrt{s_{n}}\varepsilon^{2}/3).

Then Lemma
S-31
applied with this
\rho_{n}
and
\lambda=\min(1-(1+\varepsilon)s_{n}^{-1/2},\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-\varepsilon)
further yields

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\Big(\lambda\wedge\frac{\rho_{n}\lambda}{1+\rho_{n}\lambda}\Big)(1-\exp(-\sqrt{s_{n}}\varepsilon^{2}/3)).

In view of the fact that
\lambda=\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-\varepsilon
for
n
large enough,

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-\varepsilon+o(1).

The left side does not depend on
\varepsilon
, so taking the limit as this tends to zero yields the lower bound on the minimax risk obtained already as Theorem
4
(first bullet).

Lemma S-31
.

For any procedure
\varphi
, any
\theta\in\ell_{0}[s]
with
s\geq 1
, and any
\lambda<1
and
\rho>0
,
