\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\left(\lambda\wedge\frac{\rho\lambda}{1+\rho\lambda}\right)P_{\theta}(L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\lambda\rho s).

Proof.

If
\lambda\leq 0
the bound holds trivially. Otherwise, let us write
D_{n}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)
for the total number of rejections, and denote by
s_{\theta}
the number of nonzero coefficients of
\theta
. Define

Q(X)=Q(X,\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\frac{\varphi_{i}(X)}{1\vee D_{n}(X)}+\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\frac{1-\varphi_{i}(X)}{s}\right\},

so that
\mathfrak{R}(\theta,\varphi)\geq E_{\theta}Q(X)
, using
s_{\theta}\leq s
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{n}=\{D_{n}(X)\leq(1+\delta)s\}
, for
\delta>0
. On the one hand, since
1\vee D_{n}(X)\leq(1+\delta)s
on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,

\displaystyle Q(X)\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\frac{\varphi_{i}(X)}{(1+\delta)s}+\frac{\rho}{\rho}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\frac{1-\varphi_{i}(X)}{s}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}\wedge\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\frac{1}{s}L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi).

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,

\displaystyle Q(X)\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\frac{\varphi_{i}(X)}{D_{n}(X)}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(X)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{\theta_{i}\neq 0\}\varphi_{i}(X)}{D_{n}(X)}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}\frac{D_{n}(X)-s_{\theta}}{D_{n}(X)}\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}\frac{D_{n}(X)-s}{D_{n}(X)}\geq\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}.

Combining the previous bounds and setting
\mathcal{C}_{n}=\{L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\lambda\rho s\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle Q(X)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}\wedge\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\frac{1}{s}L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}+\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}\wedge\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\rho\lambda\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}+\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\geq\left[\left(\frac{\lambda\rho}{1+\delta}\wedge\lambda\right)\wedge\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\right]\mathbf{1}\{L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\lambda\rho s\},

where the second line uses that
1\geq\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}
and uses the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
to bound
L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)
from below, and the third line that
\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}+\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{c}\}=1
.
Setting
\delta=\lambda\rho>0
and taking the expectation under
P_{\theta}
on both sides of the inequality leads to the result.
∎

Recall the definition (
21
) of
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
as comprising vectors
\theta\in\ell_{0}[s_{n}]
with non-zero components of absolute values at least
a_{j}
, and the definition (
22
) of
\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})=s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}F_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})
.

Theorem S-13

(Lower bound for weighted classification losses)
.

Grant Assumption
1
1
and fix
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{s_{n}}
. Then for any integer sequence
\rho=\rho_{n}
satisfying

1\leq\rho\leq(n/2s_{n})\overline{F}_{0}(a_{n}^{*}-\delta_{n})-1,

any
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
and any
s_{n}>4
, we have

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}P_{\theta}\Big(L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\min\big(1-\tfrac{1+\varepsilon}{\sqrt{s_{n}}},(\Lambda_{n}(\bm{a})-\varepsilon)\big)\rho s_{n}\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-\sqrt{s_{n}}\varepsilon^{2}/3).

where the infimum is over all possible multiple testing procedures
\varphi=\varphi(X)
.

Note that the right side in the statement of Theorem
S-13
only depends on
s_{n},\varepsilon
, so that one may optimise the left side with respect to
\rho
. In particular, in view of Assumption
1
, we may choose some sequence
\rho\to\infty
, or take
\rho=1
. A version holds under Assumption
1
2
; we omit the details.

Some ideas of the proof are inspired from
[
46
]
, who derived an asymptotically sharp bound for the minimax in-probability risk in terms of the quadratic loss and Gaussian noise. Weighted classification loss is, like quadratic loss, a sum over coordinates, so one can split the global loss into blocks and define a least favourable prior in a similar way as for the former. There are two important differences: firstly, our working with weighted classification losses leads to the study of different Bayes estimators, and secondly, we shall study the Bayes risk globally instead of reducing the problem to the study of Bayes risks over blocks. The latter turns out to be necessary, as unlike for the quadratic loss, there is no “concentration” for the loss over a given block for (weighted or unweighted) classification losses.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
4
; we give a stand-alone proof here.
Since
L_{\rho}\geq 0
, there is nothing to prove if
\Lambda_{n}\leq\varepsilon
, so assume that
\Lambda_{n}>\varepsilon
.
Let
q=\lfloor n/s_{n}\rfloor
and let
n^{\prime}=qs_{n}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}}
be the set of all prior distributions on
\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})
. For any
\lambda\in(0,1)
we have

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}P_{\theta}\left[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\lambda\rho s_{n}\right]\geq\sup_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}}}\inf_{\varphi}P_{\pi}\left[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\lambda\rho s_{n}\right],

(S-69)

where
P_{\pi}
denotes the distribution of
(\theta,X)
in the Bayesian setting
\theta\sim\pi
and
X\,|\,\theta\sim P_{\theta}
. Let us define a specific prior
\pi
as a product prior over
s_{n}
blocks of consecutive coordinates
Q_{1}=\{1,2,\ldots,q\},Q_{2}=\{q+1,\ldots,2q\},\ldots,Q_{s_{n}}=\{(s_{n}-1)q+1,\ldots,n^{\prime}\}
. We write
Q_{\infty}
for the (possibly empty) set
\{n^{\prime}+1,\dots,n\}
. Let
\beta[m,a]
denote the vector of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, with coordinates defined, for
1\leq i,m\leq q
and
a>0
, by

(\beta[m,a])_{i}=\begin{cases}&a\quad\ \quad\>\text{if }i=m;\\
&0\quad\ \quad\>\>\;\text{if }i\neq m.\end{cases}

In words,
\beta[m,a]\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is the
1
-sparse vector with its only nonzero coordinate, at position
m
, equalling
a
.
Over each block
Q_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq s_{n}
, one takes the following prior:
first draw an integer
I_{j}
from the uniform distribution
\mathcal{U}(Q_{j})
over the block
Q_{j}
and next set
\beta_{I_{j}}=a_{j}

as above.
That is,
\pi_{j}
generates
\beta^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, to be identified with
(\beta_{i})_{i\in Q_{j}}
, according to

I_{j}\sim\mathcal{U}(Q_{j}),\qquad\beta^{j}\,|\,I_{j}\sim\delta_{\beta[I_{j}\,\text{mod}\,q,a_{j}]},

(S-70)

with ‘mod
q
’ meaning modulo the integer
q
.
If
Q_{\infty}
is non-empty, set
\beta^{\infty}\sim\delta_{0}
.
By definition,
\pi
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Using the fact that the classification loss is a sum of losses over all coordinates, one may rewrite
L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}L^{j}+L^{\infty}
, with
L^{j}
the contribution of block
Q_{j}
. For
1\leq j\leq s_{n}
, we note that for
\theta
sampled from
\pi
,

L^{j}=L^{j}_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)=\rho\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{I_{j}}=0\}+\sum_{i\in Q_{j},\,i\neq I_{j}}\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}\neq 0\}.

Then for any integer
\rho
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
, noting that
L^{\infty}\geq 0
we see that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left[L_{\rho}(\theta,\varphi)\geq\lambda\rho s_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\geq P_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}L^{j}\geq\lambda\rho s_{n}\right]=1-P_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}L^{j}<\lambda\rho s_{n}\right].

For a multiple testing procedure
\varphi
, let
A^{\varphi}=\{i:\,\varphi_{i}(X)\neq 0\}
denote its support and
A^{\varphi}_{j}=A^{\varphi}\cap Q_{j}
its support within the
j
th block.
Let us consider the event
\mathcal{E}
defined as

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(\varphi)=\mathcal{E}(\varphi,\rho,\lambda)=\left\{L^{1}+\cdots+L^{s_{n}}<\lambda\rho s_{n}\right\}.

To complete the proof, it is enough to bound
\sup_{\varphi}P_{\pi}[\mathcal{E}]
from above for a suitable
\lambda
. Let us further define

N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{s_{n}}\mathbf{1}\{I_{j}\in A_{j}^{\varphi},\ \lvert A_{j}^{\varphi}\rvert\leq\rho\}.

One next notes that the following inequality holds:

L^{1}+\cdots+L^{s_{n}}\geq(s_{n}-N_{\varphi}(X,\theta))\rho.

(S-71)

To check this, it suffices to verify that for any index
j
for which the indicator equals
0
in the sum defining
N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)
, the corresponding loss
L^{j}
is at least
\rho
. The latter is true because if
I_{j}\notin A_{j}^{\varphi}
we have
\rho\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{I_{j}}=0\}=\rho
, and if
\lvert A_{j}^{\varphi}\rvert\geq\rho+1
then
\mathbf{1}\{\varphi_{i}\neq 0\}
equals
1
at least
\rho
times for
i\in Q_{j},i\neq I_{j}
. This leads to the desired inequality, which itself further implies that, for any integer
\rho\geq 1
,

\mathcal{E}\subset\{N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)\geq s_{n}-\lambda s_{n}\}.

We wish to bound
P_{\pi}[\mathcal{E}]=E_{\pi}(P_{\pi}[\mathcal{E}\,|\,X])
from above uniformly in
\varphi
, where
E_{\pi}
denotes the (Bayesian) expectation under
P_{\pi}
, and it suffices to bound from above

\sup_{\varphi}E_{\pi}P_{\pi}[\mathcal{E}\,|\,X]\leq\sup_{\varphi}E_{\pi}P_{\pi}[N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}\,|\,X].

Conditional on
X
, the distribution of
N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)
is Poisson-binomial, that is, the law of a sum of
s_{n}
independent Bernoulli variables
Z_{j}
with parameters
p^{\varphi}_{j}(X)
given by

p^{\varphi}_{j}(X)=\mathbf{1}\{\lvert A_{j}^{\varphi}\rvert\leq\rho\}P_{\pi}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{\varphi}\,|\,X].

Let us now investigate the posterior distribution
P_{\pi}[\cdot\,|\,X]
, restricted to coordinates
\theta_{i},

i\in\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}=\cup_{j=1}^{s_{n}}Q_{j}
. By definition the prior distribution is a product over the blocks
Q_{j}
. The model, that is the law of
X\,|\,\theta
, is also of product form, so by Bayes’ formula the posterior
\pi[\cdot\,|\,X]
is a product
\otimes_{j=1}^{s_{n}}\pi_{j}[\cdot\,|\,X^{j}]
, where
X^{j}
denotes the observations
(X_{i}:i\in Q_{j})
over the block
Q_{j}
. Writing
w_{i}^{j}(X)=P_{\pi}(I_{j}=i\mid X^{j})
for the posterior probability that
I_{j}=i
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{j}[\cdot\,|\,X^{j}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}^{j}(X)\delta_{\beta[i\text{ mod }q,a_{j}]},

\displaystyle w_{i}^{j}(X)=

\displaystyle\frac{f_{a_{j}}(X_{i})/f_{0}(X_{i})}{\sum_{k\in Q_{j}}f_{a_{j}}(X_{k})/f_{0}(X_{k})}=\frac{h(X_{i},a_{j})}{\sum_{k\in Q_{j}}h(X_{k},a_{j})},

\displaystyle h(x,a):=

\displaystyle f_{a}(x)/f_{0}(x).

By definition,
P_{\pi}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{\varphi}\,|\,X]=\sum_{i\in A_{j}^{\varphi}}h(X_{i},a_{j})/\sum_{k\in Q_{j}}h(X_{k},a_{j}).
Among (frequentist) estimators
\varphi(X)
such that
\lvert A_{j}^{\varphi}\rvert=\#\{i:\varphi_{i}(X)\neq 0\}\leq\rho
, this expression is maximal for any estimator whose support
A_{j}^{\varphi}
over the block
Q_{j}
is equal to the set
A_{j}^{X}
consisting of indices
i_{1}^{j},\cdots,i_{\rho}^{j}
corresponding to the
\rho
largest observations among
(X_{i}:i\in Q_{j})
; to see this, recall that Assumption (
1
) tells us that
h(X_{k},a_{j})
is increasing in
X_{k}
.

Recalling
p^{\varphi}_{j}(X)=\mathbf{1}\{\lvert A_{j}^{\varphi}\rvert\leq\rho\}P_{\pi}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{\varphi}\,|\,X]
, for any procedure
\varphi
and all
j
, we have

p^{\varphi}_{j}(X)\leq P_{\pi}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{X}\,|\,X]=\pi_{j}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{X}\,|\,X^{j}]=:p_{j}^{*}(X^{j}).

Write
\mathbf{p^{\varphi}}
for the vector of
p^{\varphi}_{j}
’s, and similarly for
\mathbf{p^{*}}
. Lemma
S-25
implies that, given
X
,
\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{p^{*}}]
stochastically dominates
\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{p^{\varphi}}]
(see the definition of the Poisson-Binomial distribution just before Lemma
S-25
), so that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}[N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}\,|\,X]=P_{\pi}[\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{p^{\varphi}}]\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}\,|\,X]

\displaystyle\qquad\ \leq P_{\pi}[\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{p^{*}}]\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}\,|\,X],

Let us note that
E_{\pi}p_{j}^{*}(X^{j})=P_{\pi}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{X}]
and

P_{\pi}[I_{j}\in A_{j}^{X}]=P_{\pi}[X_{I_{j}}\sim f_{a_{j}}\text{ belongs to the $\rho$ largest among coordinates of }X^{j}].

Lemma
S-7
(coupled with the fact that
I_{j}
has a uniform distribution over
Q_{j}
and is independent of
X
) tells us that the probability on the right is
p_{j}:=\overline{F}_{a_{j}}(a_{n}^{*})+\eta_{j,n}
, for some sequences
\eta_{j,n}=\eta_{j,n}(\bm{a})
tending to zero as
n\to\infty
, uniformly in
j\leq s_{n}
as
n\to\infty
. Write
\eta_{n}=s_{n}^{-1}\sum_{j\leq s_{n}}\eta_{j,n}.

This implies that the parameters
p_{j}^{*}(X^{j})
of the Poisson-binomial

\operatorname{PBin}[\mathbf{p^{*}}]
have expectations given by the vector
\bm{p}=(p_{j})_{j\leq n}
in the Bayesian model under
E_{\pi}
. Taking the expectation under
E_{\pi}
in the last but one display and using Lemma
S-26
leads to

\sup_{\varphi}P_{\pi}[N_{\varphi}(X,\theta)\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}]\leq P[\operatorname{PBin}[\bm{p}]\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}].

Note that
\operatorname{PBin}(\bm{p})
has mean
s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n})
.

If
1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n}\geq s_{n}^{-1/2}
, define
\lambda
by

1-\lambda=(1+\varepsilon)(1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n}),

which, recalling that we have excluded already the case
\Lambda_{n}\leq\varepsilon
, indeed has a solution
\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\varepsilon,\bm{a})\in(0,1)
, at least for
n
large enough that
\eta_{n}<\varepsilon^{2}/2\leq\varepsilon\Lambda_{n}/2
. We apply Bernstein’s inequality for deviations of the Poisson Binomial distribution from its mean (Lemma
S-27
) to obtain

P[\operatorname{PBin}[\bm{p}]\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}]\leq\exp(-s_{n}(1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n}))\varepsilon^{2}/3)\leq\exp(-s_{n}^{1/2}\varepsilon^{2}/3).

If instead
1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n}<s_{n}^{-1/2}
, we set

1-\lambda=(1+\varepsilon)s_{n}^{-1/2}

and Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
S-27
), with the upper bound
s_{n}^{1/2}
for the variance and expectation of
\operatorname{PBin}[\bm{p}]
, yields

P[\operatorname{PBin}[\bm{p}]\geq(1-\lambda)s_{n}]\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}s_{n}^{1/2}/2}{1+\varepsilon/3}\Big)\leq\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}s_{n}^{1/2}/3).

Combining, and returning to (
S-69
), we deduce that

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta(\bm{a},s_{n})}P_{\theta}\Big(L_{\rho}(\theta,T)\geq(1-\lambda)\rho s_{n}\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-\sqrt{s_{n}}\varepsilon^{2}/3),

for
1-\lambda=\max\Big\{\tfrac{1+\varepsilon}{\sqrt{s_{n}}},(1+\varepsilon)\big(1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n}\big)\Big\}
.
Noting that
1-(1+\varepsilon)(1-\Lambda_{n}+\eta_{n})\geq\Lambda_{n}-\varepsilon,
at least for
n
large, concludes the proof.
∎

S-13
Further discussion and comparison of Assumption
1
with
[
41
]

The closest previous model to ours that we know of comes from
[
41
]
, in which the authors consider data defined for some function
f
by

\displaystyle X_{i}=\begin{cases}W_{i}&\theta_{i}=0\\
f(W_{i})&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle W=(W_{i}:i\leq n)\sim\mathbb{P}_{n}.

This helped inspire our Assumption
1
but there are some key differences:

1.

They do not assume
\mathbb{P}_{n}
is of product form, i.e. the
X_{i}
need not be independent.

2.

They have a single function
f
controlling the non-null signals, in contrast to our setting where each non-null
X_{i}
has its own
\theta_{i}
(though they could in principle use the non-iid nature of the
W_{i}
to partially accommodate this).

3.

In place of Assumption
1
, they assume that
f
is non-decreasing and
f(w)\geq w
for all
w
in the support of the variables
W_{i}
. They assume the distribution of each
W_{i}
is non-atomic.

4.

Their analysis is for ‘top
K
’ procedures only.

The key advantage of their assumptions is that they can accommodate non-independent data. For their results to be practical, they assume in Section 4.1.1 that some concentration inequality holds. The key reason we need independence is for concentration arguments, so it is possible that our results could be generalised to hold under some version of their concentration assumptions.

Similarly, while they do not assume anything of the form (
16
) and (
17
), we need this only to ensure we get matching upper bounds to the lower bounds, which they do not target.

Finally, the key advantage of our assumptions is that we do not need to restrict our attention to top-
K
procedures: we are able to prove that procedures based on absolute value thresholding are asymptotically optimal under (
18
)–(
19
).
In contrast, note that their assumption that
f
is increasing and
f(w)\geq w
does not justify that top-
K
procedures will be optimal. Indeed, if
f(w)=\lceil w\rceil
, then (due to the non-atomicity assumption) the optimal procedure will not be a top-
K
procedure but rather will be

(\varphi_{i}(X))_{i\leq n}=(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\})_{i\leq n},

and will have
\FDR
and
\FNR
both equal to zero. Note also that top-
K
procedures are provably suboptimal for boundary data in our setting: see Remark
5
and Corollary
S-3
.
